SELECT` over `generate_series` as the owner, with the foreign keys and the user triggers of the three tables off for the writing session, as `trigger_scale.rs` does (the per-row guards and counters are what make a bulk write minutes long); the fleet is then given a schedule for every trigger by the same statements, so that the check under the handover's lock (which must look at every trigger and find none) is measured on the state the handover needs, at 5,000 to 100,000 triggers; the state after an upgrade (no schedule for any trigger) is written the same way from the trigger a real publication made for each of the two real tenants, with the guards on, so that the reconciliation really writes the schedules; the engine's own loop (`loops::run_triggers`, its pacing of a pause after a round that wrote) then works through the backlog for a fixed time and the writes a second are measured.
- **D2112** (2026-11-09, T018-072) **No production code and no ceiling was changed by this task.** The findings that follow from the data are registered as gaps (G2043 to G2050), each with what would change it, and each is left to a decision of its own: a ceiling is changed only by a decision that quotes the data, and none of the changes the data suggests (the pause of the batch loop, the paging of the restore of a reset, the plan of the handover check, the runbooks) is small enough to be made, and tested in both roles, inside a task that measures. The one decision about a ceiling that the data supports is to leave `RESET_PREFIX_EVENTS` at 5,000: the source row is locked for 4.65 s at that size, and 20,000 must not be accepted before the restore is paged (G2046).
- **D2113** (2026-11-09, T018-072) **The attempts that did not count are kept out of the report and named in it.** The first launch at 10,000 schedules (stopped by the default active-run quota, G2045), the trigger backlog scenario twice (an assertion of the harness that compared the writes of the rounds with a count that is capped at 100,000 and with a count that excludes the repairs of the two triggers a real publication made), the first steady-state scenario (replaced by one that also measures the converged fleet, because the check under the handover's lock turned out to depend on the size) and the first three repetitions of the firing at the same instant (replaced to record the outcomes of the actions) are not in the tables; the report says so under "How the run went", with the difference the replaced firing numbers made (within 6%). The bounds were not changed by any of them.
- **D2116** (2026-11-09, T018-072 follow-up, G2043) **The batch loop sleeps until the soonest slot of a job, never longer than its poll.** **[Amended the same day by D2123 to D2126, after two reviews: the statement that finds the slot is folded into the scan of the due jobs, which reads the database's clock once; only a running job's slot counts; `MIN_SLOT_WAKE` is private; a due job that the executor finds not due yet comes back in 10 ms. What follows is the first design and its measurements; where it says `BatchStore::next_slot_in`, read the second half of `due_jobs`.]** *The defect:* after an item a job's next slot is `1 / rate` ahead (50 ms at the default 20 a second), the round that follows finds nothing due, did no work, and the loop rested its whole poll (1,000 ms): one item a second. *The change:* `BatchRound` has a field `next_due_in: Option<Duration>`, set by `BatchPass::round` **only for a round that did nothing and met no refusal and no failure**, from a new port method `BatchStore::next_slot_in(shards)` (`PgBatchStore`: one statement, `unnest(shards)` cross-joined with a `LATERAL (... ORDER BY next_slot_at LIMIT 1)` over `batch_jobs_due_idx (shard, next_slot_at)` for the states `SELECTING`, `RUNNING` and `CANCELLING`, `next_slot_at > statement_timestamp()`; the answer is `min(slot) - clock_timestamp()` **by the database's clock**, so no clock of this process is involved, clamped between 0 and a day). `loops::run_batches` rests `min(jittered(poll), max(next_due_in, MIN_SLOT_WAKE))` (`idle_pause`; `MIN_SLOT_WAKE` is 1 ms): the poll keeps its jitter and is still the longest the loop rests, a slot is not jittered (it is durable and each job's own: the jitter only keeps replicas that started together apart), a slot that has come or comes within a hair is still a wait of 1 ms and never a loop with no pause, and the rest is `stop_during`, so a stop ends it. A round that did work is followed at once, as before, and does not ask. *Its cost, said:* the statement is at most 64 index probes (one for each shard the replica holds), each reading the first entry of the index after the present (0.34 ms with no job at all in the plan I looked at, `Index Only Scan` with `Heap Fetches: 0`); it is asked **only by a round that found nothing due**, which is one round a poll for an engine with no work and, for a job that applies items, one idle round after each item (a clock read, the due scan and this statement); no round that did work asks, and the round does not scan the active jobs. The jobs that the rest of the loop already left to the poll stay there: a selection waiting for a transaction and a job whose items are all deferred have their slot in the past, so they are not the hint, and a finished job's last slot, which the last item moves ahead too, is not counted (the state filter). *Not changed:* the executor (never catches up in a burst, one item of a job a step), the order of the due scan (`next_slot_at, id`), the fairness, the ceilings of a round, the fences, the rate. *Tests, red first:* 7 unit tests of the loop under `tokio::time::pause` (the tokio `test-util` feature is a dev-dependency of the engine): a round that says "50 ms" wakes the loop at 50 ms and not at the poll (it slept 1.13 s before); no hint sleeps the poll jittered; a slot an hour ahead is still the poll; a round that worked is followed at once; a stop during the wait ends the loop; 2,000 rounds that answer "now" take at least 1,999 ms (no loop with no pause: with no floor they would take none); the pure function of the pause with a fixed entropy. 3 integration tests in both roles (`batch_pass.rs`): the store for a job 30 s ahead (29 to 30 s), for a shard that is not the replica's, for none and for a finished job; the pass (a round that applied items says nothing, the idle one after it says 1 s or less for a job at one item a second, and nothing once the job is over); and **the engine's own loop with a poll of 10 s completes the four items of a lone job at the default rate within 5 s** (it timed out at 5 s before the change; the earliest a jittered poll of 10 s would wake it is 8 s). *Hand mutants:* the loop ignores the hint (killed by the wake-up test), no floor (killed by the two tests that name it), the pass does not ask (red before the method was written: the three integration tests), the state filter of the statement removed (killed by the store test and the pass test: a finished job's slot was the answer), the pass asks also after a round that worked (killed by the pass test); each restored and the tests green again. *Measured* (the benchmark's own scenario of the lone job, release build, 3 of 3 repetitions, 120 runs at the default rate): **18.3 and 18.4 items a second in two runs of three repetitions (the machine at a load of 20 to 34 and then of 5), against 0.97 to 0.98 before; the gap between two items 54.4 to 54.6 ms at the median against 1,036 ms** (the 1.6 items a second that are missing from 20 are the round that asks for the slot and the wake-up, about 4 ms an item: G2051; the addendum of the report has the load of each repetition). The bound "reaches 80% of the rate it asked for" held in 6 of 6 repetitions (it was broken in 3 of 3). The scenarios that must not change were rerun: sixteen jobs (73.5 items a second in all, the last job's end over the first's 1.00), one job of 900 runs beside fifteen of 30 (72.0, 1.04), a lone job at the highest rate (73.1); the fairness bounds held in 3 of 3 and the capacity bounds stay broken as before.
- **D2117** (2026-11-09, T018-072 follow-up, G2049) **The drift view looks the schedule up for each trigger, so its cost does not depend on the plan the statistics give it.** *What I found* (`EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)` of `SELECT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM schedule_trigger_drift)` on the benchmark's own database at 10,000 triggers, PostgreSQL 16.15, the same image and settings as the report): the tables **were** analyzed, after the bulk insert and again after the stragglers were converged (`last_analyze` seconds before, `n_mod_since_analyze` 0 and 2), so the bulk insert's missing statistics are **no part** of it. The plan was `Nested Loop Left Join ... Join Filter: (s.trigger_id = t.id)` over a `Materialize` of a sequential scan of `schedules`, `Rows Removed by Join Filter: 99990000`, 30,497 buffers, 3.1 s with `ANALYZE`. The estimate was `rows=6069500` for the outer join: 10,000 triggers times **610 rows for `schedule_trigger_handover`**, a one-row table that is never analyzed (`reltuples = -1`: the planner's default for a table it knows nothing of is 10 pages) times 0.995 (the planner's estimate of `CASE ... IS NOT NULL`). An `EXISTS` is planned for its first row, so with six million estimated rows the cost of a plan is its start-up cost, and a nested loop with no start-up beat the hash join (423 to start) whatever its total (1.7 million). **`ANALYZE schedule_trigger_handover` alone, nothing else, brought the same statement to 21.5 ms with `Index Scan using schedules_trigger_uniq` (60,174 buffers).** At 15,000 triggers the estimates cross and the planner picks the index plan, which is why the cost looked like it followed the size. In a deployment the singleton is as unanalyzed as here (a row inserted by a migration and updated once). *What was changed* (in place in `20270114000000_trigger_handover`, never applied outside tests and not released; its `down` is as it was): the view reads the schedule by `LEFT JOIN LATERAL (SELECT ... FROM schedules x LEFT JOIN schedule_revisions r ... WHERE x.trigger_id = t.id LIMIT 1) s ON true` (a trigger has at most one schedule: `schedules_trigger_uniq`) and the singleton by a scalar subquery inside the `CASE` (an uncorrelated `InitPlan`, evaluated once, and the table has exactly one row: a primary key on a boolean with a check, no delete, no truncate), so the plan is **an index probe for each trigger** and no statistic, missing or stale, offers the planner the join that it rescanned. The form does not by itself rule out a per-trigger sequential scan with a limit (PostgreSQL does not take a partial unique index as proof that a trigger has one schedule, and with no statistics it would estimate about half a percent of the rows): **`trigger_plan.rs` is what holds the plan**, by its assertions on the shape of the plan in three states of the statistics, and it would fail on that plan too. The view stays the ONE definition read by the reconciliation (`DRIFT`), the count of the gauge and the dry run (`schedule_trigger_convergence`) and the check under the lock (`schedule_trigger_unconverged`): their SQL is unchanged. *Not done, and why:* an `ANALYZE` by the command before it takes the lock, or in the migration: it would make the form a plan the planner may still choose badly for another reason and would take a lock the command does not need to take; the singleton's statistic is now irrelevant. *Tests, red first, in both roles:* `trigger_plan.rs`: at 10,000 triggers never analyzed and at 5,000 analyzed before they were written (the singleton unanalyzed in both) the plan of the check holds no sequential scan of `schedules` or `schedule_revisions` and uses `schedules_trigger_uniq`, and the check answers within a second (a machine ten times slower than this one still does; the old plan was 2.85 s here), in three states of the statistics each (none, the big tables analyzed, everything analyzed); and the view lists, with the previous definition kept in the test as an oracle, **exactly the same rows, columns and kinds for 1,700 triggers in seventeen classes (eleven here, six more by D2128)** (planned, no schedule, deleted, three kinds of respec, no revision row, paused, not planned, exhausted, and the two precedences: deleted before respec, respec before paused) in the states `LEGACY` and `HANDED_OVER` (the latter in a rolled-back transaction: the state has no reverse), the count of the gauge equal (1,300) and the check true. *Hand mutants:* the expression compared at `{cron,1}` (killed by the oracle test), the pause listed in the state `HANDED_OVER` and not `LEGACY` (killed). *Measured* (the benchmark's scenarios `triggers-steady-small`, `triggers-steady` and `triggers-steady-mid`, release build, 3 repetitions each, a converged fleet, p50 of the check under `EXISTS`, before → after): 5,000 triggers 1,009 → 10 ms; 10,000 2,853 → 19.7 ms; 15,000 28.0 → 28.2 ms; 20,000 43.5 → 39.2 ms; 50,000 111 → 112 ms; 100,000 280 → 263 ms (the slowest sample 344 → 335 ms: over the 300 ms that was asked for by 12%, and what the old index plan gave there); the windows and the whole sweep of 100,000 triggers are faster (86 → 48 ms a window, 9.1 → 2.4 s), the convergence count is slower up to 20,000 triggers (10 → 19 ms at 10,000) and faster from 50,000 up, and an unbounded `count(*)` of the view, which nothing in the product issues, is 1.3 to 2 times slower. The statement timeout of 4 s and the lock timeout of 2 s hold with a margin of two orders of magnitude to 100,000 triggers; the cost is now linear in the number of triggers (G2052).
- **D2118** (2026-11-09, G2048) **The reconciliation's "100 writes a second" is a ceiling, and the documents say "at most" and the measured range.** The pause after a round that wrote (`TriggerRound::pause_after`) is added to the time of the round, so the pace is lower than 100: measured with two tenants and the engine's own loop for 60 s, **per size**: 100,000 triggers 62 a second under the default settings (27 minutes) and 77 with the per-tenant cap of a round raised in code to 100 (21 minutes); 10,000 triggers 30 and 69 a second (6 and 2 minutes). The two sizes differ under the defaults because the sweep over a small backlog restarts and a round that finds nothing to write is followed by the poll of 5 s (7 or 8 such rounds in the 60 s at 10,000, against none that mattered at 100,000): read from the counts of the rounds in the report, not tested alone. (An earlier text said "30 to 62 a second ... 27 minutes": the 30 is the 10,000-trigger scenario and at 30 a second 100,000 triggers would take 55 minutes.) Corrected: ADR-0083 (decision 7, the consequences and the estimate), `docs/runbooks/schedules.md` (the reconciliation paragraph and step 3 of 6.2), the notes of D2047 and G2023 in this file, and the doc comments of `TriggerRound::WRITES_PER_SECOND` and `TriggerSettings::MAX_BATCH`. The pause of the pass that expires `CANCEL_SOURCE` entries (`ResetRound::pause_after`, "a hundred a second") was not measured and is not touched.
- **D2119** (2026-11-09, G2044, G2045) **The capacity of one replica and the quota of active runs are in the schedules runbook, as measurements.** `docs/runbooks/schedules.md` 4.1 has the table of the report (a burst of N schedules due together is late by about N / 61 s at the last, two replicas about halve it, spread over a minute 1,000 are kept up with and 10,000 are not, the round ceilings are not a lever), says in its first lines that the figures are one machine on one day and not promises (NFR-018-005) and names what was not measured; it also names the default quota of 10,000 active runs, `ADMISSION_RETRY`, the metric and what an operator sees (a lag that grows without end and `SagatSchedulerLate` that fires on it and not on its cause), and section 3 points to it. "About three replicas or more for 10,000 per-minute schedules" is an extrapolation and is called one.
- **D2120** (2026-11-09, G2046, G2047) **The reset runbook says what the lock time and the replay cost mean for the person who runs it** (`docs/runbooks/run-reset.md` 2.1): a reset holds its source row for about 0.93 ms an event (4.65 s at the 5,000 the ceiling accepts, 0.15 to 0.24 s inside the 5 s statement timeout of the pool), the points, the preview and the reset replay the whole journal at a cost more than linear in its length (9 ms at 1,002 events, 89 ms at 5,002, 1.3 s at 20,002), a refusal past the ceiling is cheap (46 ms), and the ceiling is not to be raised before the restore is paged. No code changed: the restore is still one statement an event and the replay is still whole (G2046, G2047 stay open).
- **D2121** (2026-11-09, G2043, G2044) **The batch runbook says what a job gets** (`docs/runbooks/batch-operations.md` 2.1): the rate is a ceiling; a lone job at the default rate now applies 18.4 items a second (1.0 before D2116); one replica applies about 73 to 75 items a second to all its jobs together and shares them fairly (sixteen jobs end within 1% of each other, none waited more than 0.4 s for its first item, a job of 900 runs beside fifteen of 30 held no small one back), so sixteen jobs at the default rate get about 4.5 a second each and a job at the highest rate about 75, not 200 (the two bounds of the benchmark that stay broken are the replica's capacity, not the loop: G2044); how long a job takes is its runs over `min(rate, 73 / the active jobs on the replica)`. The batch pass on two replicas was not measured (G2050).
- **D2122** (2026-11-09) **The benchmark is rerun for what changed, and its report gets an addendum.** The original tables, bounds and findings of `tests/performance/reports/schedules-bulk-operations.md` are not rewritten (D2106, D2112: they are what the unchanged code did); the section "After the fixes of 2026-11-09" has the scenarios rerun with the same `run.sh`, binary profile, database image and settings, three repetitions each, the load of the machine before and after each (it was 20 to 34 for the first run of the lone job, after a rebuild, so that scenario was run again at a load of 5, and both are shown), and says which bounds that were broken now hold and which still do not and why. The scenarios rerun are the ones that the changes touch (the lone job at the default rate, the checks of the handover at 5,000, 10,000, 15,000, 20,000, 50,000 and 100,000 triggers) and the ones that guard what must not change (the sixteen jobs at the default rate, the lone job at the highest rate, one big job beside fifteen small ones: fairness and the replica's capacity). The firing, the claim, the reset, the selection and the drain were not rerun: nothing they read was changed. The windows of the reconciliation read the view, so the scenario that drains a backlog (`triggers-backlog`, about six and a half minutes a repetition) was run **once** (N = 1, a point and not a median, and the addendum says so): the count and the first window of a backlog are three to four times faster at 100,000 triggers and the drain is at the same pace (66.7 and 77.3 writes a second at 100,000, 25 and 21 minutes).
- **D2123** (2026-11-09, G2043, the correctness and the architecture review, A1) **The slot ahead is found in the statement that finds the due jobs, at the instant that statement reads the clock, so a slot cannot come between the two and be lost.** The first design asked for the slot in a second statement (`next_slot_in`, with the bound `next_slot_at > statement_timestamp()`): the scan could find a job's slot 1 ms ahead, the round do nothing, and by the time the second statement ran the slot had come, so it answered nothing and the loop slept its whole poll while an item was due (reasoned from the code by both reviewers, not reproduced: the exposure is highest at 100 to 200 items a second, where the cost of an item is near the slot, and the benchmark at 20 a second did not show it). *Now:* `BatchStore::due_jobs` returns `DueJobs { jobs, next_slot_in }` from **one statement**: a CTE `t0` reads `clock_timestamp()` once; a job is due when its slot is not after `t0`, and the slot ahead is the first one after `t0` over the running jobs of each shard (one index probe a shard, 64 at the most, `Index Cond: (shard = s.shard) AND (next_slot_at > $t0)` on `batch_jobs_due_idx`, checked with `EXPLAIN` on a scratch database), measured at the end of the statement by the same clock and never below zero: **a slot that comes while the statement runs, or after it, is ahead of the instant the round was decided at, and is a zero, which the loop floors to 1 ms**. A slot that had already come at `t0` and is not due (a selection waiting for a transaction, items all deferred) is in neither answer, so such a job still cannot make the loop spin. `next_slot_in` is gone from the port. The due scan's own bound on the slot is now an index condition too (it was a volatile call per row). The scan costs 64 index probes more on every round, in the same statement, and one round trip less on an idle one. *Tests, red first:* `a_slot_that_comes_between_the_statements_of_an_idle_round_is_not_lost` (`batch_pass.rs`, with `gap_store.rs`: the real store, which makes a job's slot come right after the scan has answered and before the caller has seen the answer: no sleep, the store does the move; red against the two-statement design: "the slot that came while the round was between its statements was lost"); and `a_slot_that_comes_while_the_scan_runs_is_a_zero_and_not_lost`, which gives the statement the instant it was decided at (`PgBatchStore::due_jobs_as_of`, a seam the production call does not use: it passes none and the statement reads the clock itself) taken before a slot comes, and holds that the slot is ahead of it and zero (a lock on the table of items would hold the statement back before it reads the clock, not after: relation locks are taken before execution, which is why the first attempt, with a held lock, did not hold the window). *Hand mutants:* the bound of the slot ahead taken from `clock_timestamp()` at the later look instead of `t0` (killed by the second test; it survives the first: the window it closes is the statement's own), the two-statement design (killed by the first), the state filter removed (killed), the pass asking also after a round that worked (killed).
- **D2124** (2026-11-09, G2043, A2) **A due job that the executor finds not due yet is looked at again in 10 ms, not after the poll.** The scan decides by the database's clock; the executor decides `now < job.next_slot_at` by `DatabaseClock::now()`, which is read at the start of the round and is behind by up to half a round trip, and by whatever it has drifted since: the loop woke at the slot plus a millisecond, the scan said due, the step said idle, and the next look found a slot that had passed and said nothing, a whole poll again (a miss costs one jittered poll in production, and the integration test of the loop with a poll of 10 s and a bound of 5 s would have gone red on a slow runner). *Now:* a round that did nothing, met no refusal and no failure, and in which a step found a due job with nothing to do (`found_idle`) says `DUE_BUT_NOT_YET`, 10 ms, or the slot ahead when that is sooner (`idle_hint`, a pure function with its unit tests). It cannot spin: the job leaves the scan as soon as the engine's clock agrees; a round costs several statements; the loop never rests less than 1 ms; so it is 100 rounds a second at the most, and only while the clocks disagree. A due job that no step looked at (its shard's lease lapsed between the scan and the step) is **not** this case and is left to the poll: the keeper renews it, and looking again every 10 ms would be a hot loop for as long as it takes. *Tests, red first:* `a_due_job_that_the_executor_finds_not_due_yet_is_looked_at_again_in_a_few_milliseconds` (an executor whose clock reads an hour before the database's: a round with `due: 1, steps: 0` and no refusal; red: no hint) and three unit tests of `idle_hint` (the cases above and the rounds that worked, were refused or failed). *Hand mutant:* the hint whenever a job was due, stepped or not (killed by the lease case of the unit test).
- **D2125** (2026-11-09, G2043, A3, A4) **Hygiene of the wake-up.** (a) Only a **running** job's slot is ahead: an applied item moves it; a job in selection is due from its creation (or waits for a transaction, which no slot is for) and one that is being cancelled is moved back to its request; the `SELECTING` and `CANCELLING` entries of the state list were vestigial and are gone, with `a_slot_ahead_of_a_job_that_is_not_running_is_not_a_wake_up` holding it. (b) `MIN_SLOT_WAKE` is private to `loops.rs` (it was `pub const`, used only there and in its tests). (c) The tests that look at the time to a slot use the slowest rate the code names (`rate_milli` 1, one item in 1,000 s: `slowest`), so that no test races a slot: the one that asserted `(due, steps) == (0, 0)` after an item at one a second held only if less than a second passed between two rounds. (d) The jitter of the loop is covered by `the_idle_pause_is_the_poll_or_the_slot_whichever_comes_first` with a fixed entropy (80 %, 100 %), so removing the jitter from a slot's wake-up is held there, and the paused-time test of the poll keeps the range (800 to 1,200 ms) the system's entropy gives.
- **D2126** (2026-11-09, A, the benchmark) **The lone-job scenario is rerun after the fold, and it stays at about 18 a second.** See the second addendum of the report ("After the review fixes of 2026-11-09"): 18.3 [18.3 - 18.4] items a second at a load of 4.5 to 6.4 (the gap between two items 54.6 ms at the median) and 18.3 [18.2 - 18.3] right after the build at a load of 23 to 34, the bound "reaches 80%" held in 6 of 6; the fairness is as before (sixteen jobs 72.2 items a second in all, last over first 1.01; one big job beside fifteen small ones 72.3 and 1.05; a lone job at the highest rate 74.5).
- **D2127** (2026-11-09, G2049, B1 to B3) **The claims about the view are what the tests hold.** ADR-0083 "Enforced by" names `trigger_plan.rs` (the plan shape in three states of the statistics, the time bound, the oracle of the previous definition) and says it is run in a pull request only when a file of its target changes (`tests/AGENTS.md`). "The only plan this form has" is gone from the migration comment, D2117 and the ADR: a `LATERAL ... LIMIT 1` gives the planner a probe for each trigger and no join to rescan, but PostgreSQL does not take a partial unique index as proof of uniqueness, so a per-trigger sequential scan with a limit is not ruled out by the form; the assertions of `trigger_plan.rs` are the protection (and fail on that plan too). The measurements ("2.85 s at 10,000", "2.6 microseconds a trigger") are out of the migration comment (they go stale) and in ADR-0083 and the report. The ids in the migration and the test are D2117 (they cited D2116). The migration comment says the one behaviour that differs: with the singleton row missing (unreachable: primary key on `only_row`, ENABLE ALWAYS triggers that refuse DELETE and TRUNCATE) the old `CROSS JOIN` listed nothing and the scalar subquery lists MISSING, REVIVED and RESPEC and never PAUSED.
- **D2128** (2026-11-09, G2049, B4) **The oracle covers seventeen classes, and a mutant of each part of the CASE is killed.** `trigger_plan.rs` seeds 1,700 cron triggers in 17 classes of 100 and 50 triggers that are not cron: the eleven of D2117 and, new, an active schedule with only the nominal time missing, with only the actual time missing, with five actions left and neither time, a planned schedule with no action left, a schedule whose head is revision 2 equal to the trigger over a revision 1 that is not (the lookup reads the head), the same with the head not equal over a revision 1 that is, and the event triggers (the `t.kind = 'cron'` filter). Expected: MISSING 100, REVIVED 200, RESPEC 500, PAUSED 100 (LEGACY only), NO_NEXT_TIME 400, so 1,300 listed (1,200 once handed over). *Hand mutants, each killed and restored:* the `OR` of the two times of NO_NEXT_TIME made `AND`; the filter `t.kind = 'cron'` removed; the revision read by number (`r.revision = 1`) and not by head; (from D2117) the expression compared at `{cron,1}`, the state of the pause inverted.
- **D2129** (2026-11-09, G2048, C1, C2) **The reconciliation's pace is stated per size, and the ceilings of the other passes are called ceilings.** "30 to 77 writes a second" next to "21 to 27 minutes" did not add up: 30 a second is the 10,000-trigger scenario under the defaults, and at that pace 100,000 triggers would take 55 minutes; only the 100,000-trigger scenarios (62 and 77 a second) give 27 and 21 minutes. Stated per size in `trigger_schedules.rs`, ADR-0083, `schedules.md` and D2118: 100,000 triggers 62 a second under the defaults (27 minutes) and 77 with the per-tenant cap at 100 (21); 10,000 triggers 30 and 69 (6 and 2). The difference under the defaults is read from the report's counts of rounds (at 10,000, 7 or 8 rounds of the 60 s found nothing to write and were each followed by the poll of 5 s, which a backlog of 100,000 hardly meets), not tested alone. The pass that expires `CANCEL_SOURCE` entries pauses "at a hundred a second" the same way (the pause is added to the time of the round): `run-reset.md` (two places), `resets.rs` and `run_resets` now say a ceiling and that its pace was not measured. The stale line of the `DRIFT` statement ("joined to its schedule, not looked up") is corrected.
- **D2130** (2026-11-09, review D2) **The test of the window of `Keeper::leave` waits, with the limit of its neighbours.** `Keeper::leave` gives the shards back and then, in a second statement, withdraws the announcement; the test read the members the instant the shards read zero and failed once as the runtime role. It waits with `eventually` (`tests/support/mod.rs`: polls every 50 ms and fails after 5 s with "timed out waiting for: and leaves the replicas that count for the shard shares"), the same limit as the five other waits of that test; the window itself is milliseconds. The migration `20270114000000_trigger_handover` is edited in place: the refs that contain it are listed in the report of this task (`origin/feature/018-business-ids` only: no tag, no `develop`, no `main`), and any database that applied the old text must be recreated (sqlx validates the checksum).
- **D2131** (2026-11-09, T018-075) **The compose file passes the four switches of the feature from the environment, each `false` unless the environment says `true`, and the quickstart says which lines of `infra/docker/.env` turn them on.** `SAGAT_SCHEDULES_ENABLED`, `SAGAT_BATCHES_ENABLED` and `SAGAT_RESET_CANCEL_SOURCE_ENABLED` go to the control plane and to the engine (they must agree, ADR-0081), `SAGAT_RESET_REAPPLY_ENABLED` to the control plane only (the engine has no such setting, D1990); the other settings of the passes (`SAGAT_SCHEDULES_BATCH`, `_POLL_MS` and the rest) stay at the binaries' defaults and are not in the compose file. **No default of a binary changed** and the demo showed the effect (`the optional passes of this replica: ["schedules", "batches", "resets"]`). The lines were **not** added to `infra/docker/.env.example`: the session that did T018-075 was refused every read of a file named `.env*`, so it could neither read nor edit it without guessing its content; the four lines are in section 1 of the quickstart and the maintainer adds them (commented, off by default) when committing (G2061).
- **D2132** (2026-11-09, T018-075) **`sagat batch cancel <ID> --reason <TEXT> [--key K]` sends the JSON body the contract asks for.** `cancelBatchOperation` requires `{"reason": 8 to 2,000 characters}`; the CLI sent a POST with no body and no content type and the platform answered `400 content type must be application/json`, so no job was ever cancelled by it (the unit test of the command asserted the request line and the key and never the body, which is why it passed). Found by the demo (step 6.5), seen failing first (`variant BatchCommand::Cancel has no field named reason`, the body and the content type asserted in `a_job_is_read_listed_its_items_shown_and_cancelled`), fixed in `apps/cli/src/{args,batch}.rs`; `--reason` is required (usage error, exit 2), the length is the platform's to judge (`422` in words); the key of a cancel now depends on the reason. The usage line of `docs/reference/cli.md` was edited by hand to the same words and `cargo test -p sagat-tests --test contract_developer_experience --locked` passes (8 tests, among them `the_command_reference_is_what_the_tool_prints`, without the bless switch).
- **D2133** (2026-11-09, T018-075) **The description of a schedule lists only the runs that are still open.** `openRuns` was the schedule's own `tracked_runs`, which the pass forgets at its next step, so `sagat schedule get` said `1 open run(s)`, then `2`, for a schedule whose runs had all finished, and went on saying it for as long as the schedule was paused (a paused schedule takes no step). The firing already treats an ended run as closed (`open_run_ids`); `ScheduleService::describe` now reads the status of the tracked runs (`ScheduleStore::run_statuses`, one query, none when nothing is tracked) and returns the open ones; the stored tracking is unchanged and the pass still forgets the rest as before. Seen failing first (`firing::a_description_lists_only_the_runs_that_are_still_open_though_the_pass_has_not_forgotten_the_ended_one`: `left: [run] right: []`), green after, `rest::` and `service::` 95 passed; rerun on the stack: the database still tracked the ended run and the description said `0 open run(s)`. `ScheduleDescription.record.tracked_runs` is therefore the open ones in a description and the tracked ones everywhere else (said in the field's comment).
- **D2134** (2026-11-09, T018-075) **`quickstart.md` is rewritten to what the stack did.** Every command is the one that ran (the CLI's real flags: `schedule get <id>`, `--from/--to`, `--start-delay` in seconds, `batch --selector`, `batch items`, `batch cancel --reason`; the business-id and Signal-with-Start demos use `cart`, which stays open, because `hello-world` finishes in 0.3 s and the conflict is then a race), every "Expected" is what it showed (the run resource carries `startedBy` only, G2057; the nominal and actual times are on the action; `ALREADY_ACTED` is counted in the backfill; the note is on the schedule and not in the audit; the page says that the dev worker stops with the engine), the failure table says for each row what was shown and names the tests, and the four rows a stack cannot show are marked as not demonstrated with the reason. The runbooks were corrected where the demo showed a sentence false: `batch-operations.md` (`UPDATE` is accepted, `REPIN` refused), `run-reset.md` (`CANCEL_SOURCE` is built), and where a pointer was missing (`schedules.md`: the compose lines, the handover on the dev stack, the note not being audited, `openRuns`; `business-ids.md`: `--start-delay` is in seconds).
- **D2135** (2026-11-09, T018-075) **`verification.md` is the record of T018-075 and uses four statuses, strictly.** VERIFIED (the evidence proves the whole sentence), PARTLY (a part, with what is missing), BLOCKED (a feature that is not approved or merged), NOT DONE; a test that proves part of a criterion makes it PARTLY. Result: of the 17 criteria 6 VERIFIED and 11 PARTLY, FR-018-046 and FR-018-049 PARTLY, NFR-018-005 VERIFIED as measured. Evidence that is not the author's (the task notes, CI run `42649041664` on `345dd70e`) is named as such. The "Final gates" table is **blank on purpose**: the gates are the maintainer's (rule 17). `tasks.md` is not ticked: T018-075 has a dated note.
- **D2136** (2026-11-09, T018-075) **The run resource not carrying the schedule's provenance is recorded as a gap and the demo step as FAILED, not fixed here** (*closed on 2026-11-10 by D2141 to D2146: the step is a defect fixed*). FR-018-016 says runs "expose schedule id, nominal time, kind and revision"; `runs` has the columns (migration `20261230000200`, written at the action, immutable) and no response reads them. Exposing them is an additive field of `Run` in `openapi.yaml` (and the journal contract names `schedule {scheduleId, nominalTime, kind, revision}` on the run, `contracts/journal-and-events.md`), a change of the reference blessed from it, of the Studio's generated types and of what six SDKs parse, which the maintainer reruns as a set; the page says where to read the times today (G2057).
- **D2141** (2026-11-10, T018-010, G2057) **`Run.schedule.kind` is the kind of the action (`SCHEDULED`, `TRIGGERED`, `BACKFILL`), not the schedule's origin.** The brief of this slice said "`kind` = the schedule's origin `API`/`TRIGGER`"; the contract it cites (`contracts/journal-and-events.md`, the draft `RunAdditions` of `contracts/rest.openapi.yaml`), the stored column (`runs.schedule_action_kind`, a check of the three values) and `ScheduleActionRecord.kind` all say the action kind, and the origin is a property of the schedule (`GET …/schedules/{id}`), which a run does not copy. The contract was followed; the run has no `actualTime` (the contract does not give it one: the action has it).
- **D2142** (2026-11-10, T018-010, G2057) **The provenance is read with the run, from the columns it was created with, and is part of `RunRecord`, not of `RunInfo`.** `get` and `list` select `schedule_id`, `schedule_nominal_at`, `schedule_action_kind`, `schedule_revision` and `reset_sequence` in the statements they already ran (`RUN_SELECT`, the list query): no query per run, and the graph read (`GET /runs/{id}/graph`) has it too. `RunRecord.schedule` and `RunRecord.reset_from` are `None` for a record a command transaction reads (the rows of those do not select the columns), as `business_id` is, and a column that is there and unreadable is a stored-data error, not a silent `None`. `RunInfo` is built in dozens of tests and never changes after creation, so the fields went where `start_delayed_until` already is. `resetFrom` is `rerun_of` with `reset_sequence`: a rerun as new has the first and not the second, so it has no `resetFrom`. The list returns the same `Run` schema, so there is no separate summary to decide about. Each field is **absent, not `null`**, as the contract says (`skip_serializing_if`).
- **D2143** (2026-11-10, T018-010) **`scheduleId` and `nominalTime` are added to `data` of the `run.finished` body of a run a schedule started, in the four places that tell subscribers a run finished, through one function.** `RunFinishedWebhook.payload.data` gained the two optional properties (`RunFinishedBroker` takes the payload by reference); `events.baseline.json` was regenerated on purpose and its diff is those two properties on `RunFinishedWebhook.payload` and nothing else. `add_schedule_origin` (`infra-postgres/src/outbound.rs`) reads `schedule_id` and `schedule_nominal_at` of the run in the transaction that finishes it, **through a snapshot of the row (`to_jsonb(r.*) ->> 'schedule_id'`), as `start_delayed_until` is**, because the engine's passes must finish a run on a schema that lacks the columns (a rollback below migration `20261230000200`, a rollout whose migration has not run): the first version of the function named the columns and failed `workflow-primitives` `iteration_policy_zero::zero_policy_writer_and_downgrade_obey_both_relation_lock_orders` (762 of 763 passed: "column \"schedule_id\" does not exist" in the collection finish of a run on the rolled-back schema), the only test that finishes a run there; it passes with the snapshot. It is called by `RunTx` (`outbox_run_finished`), the collection finish (`primitives.rs`), the loop terminal path (`loop_progress.rs`) and the chain's final head (`chain_progress.rs`); a run no schedule started gets nothing added, so its body is what it was (asserted for each path, with and without a schedule, and the properties of `data` the platform writes are asserted to be declared by the document). **Cost:** one read of the run's own row by primary key per run finished, in a transaction that already holds that row (and writes about ten statements); the alternative, carrying the two columns in `RunInfo` and in the lock statements, touches every test that builds a `RunInfo` and the statements every pass of the scheduler runs, and was not worth one indexed read; it is the thing to change if a benchmark of run completion ever shows it. The read is made **also when no subscription wants the event** (`fan_out` returns early, but the body is built before it, and asking first whether a subscription exists would be a read of its own): accepted, D2153, G2071. The hint (`RunFinished` on NATS or Redis, `event_outbox.payload`) is **not** changed (the contract names the webhook and the broker body only), `businessId` is in no event (ADR-0027: a canary test searches the body a subscriber is sent and the hint), `info.version` of the document stays `0.3.0` (the release in development), and `worker.baseline.json` and `workflow.schema.json` are untouched.
- **D2144** (2026-11-10, T018-010) **Only the first run of a chain, and only a run an action of the schedule pass started, has provenance.** `continue_as_new` creates a successor that copies no schedule column, and the legacy cron pass writes none (G2063). So the `run.finished` of the final head of a chain a schedule started says nothing about the schedule, and a run a trigger-owned schedule's *legacy-pass* tick started has none; both are as the columns say today and are not guessed.
- **D2145** (2026-11-10, T018-010, FR-018-047) **The Studio says it in the header and asks for the name only when it may.** `Started by schedule <name> (scheduled | run now | backfill), nominal time <time>` is a fact of the run header (`ScheduleOrigin.tsx`); the name is read with `GET …/schedules/{id}` (cached a minute, not retried) only for a session that holds `schedule:read`, the link to `/schedules/{id}` is shown only to that session (no link leads to a page that refuses), and a schedule that cannot be read (deleted, another namespace) is called by the end of its id, with the link. A run a reset made says `Reset from run <id>; this run keeps the first N events of it` in place of `Started again from` (the source is the same run). `sagat workflow inspect` prints the same two facts in words, with the id of the schedule (no extra request: the plain output and its three requests are unchanged).
- **D2146** (2026-11-10, T018-010, FR-018-048) **The six SDKs read both objects strictly and the conformance observation shows them for two runs.** `Run.schedule` and `Run.resetFrom` are typed in every SDK (null, None or nil when absent; `kind` kept as sent; a malformed object is `PROTOCOL`), an instant keeps the language's precision (the server writes microseconds), and the runners write the two objects only when the run has them, so every golden stays what it was except two. `schedules-operate` gained two `get` steps at its end (`triggered-run`, `backfilled-run`): the run a trigger-now started and the first run a backfill started (56 lines added). `reset` changed in exactly the new field, which the whole-suite run found (the six languages agreed on it): `resetFrom {runId, sequence}` on the four observations of a run a reset made (24 lines added, `runId` equal to the `rerunOf` beside it, `sequence` 4, 4, 1 and 4). `nominalTime` is masked as `<time>` by the harness, so the golden proves the shape, the kind, the revision and that an instant is read, and the exactness of the nominal time is proved where the clock is known: the REST tests (a firing at a calculated time, the instant compared) and one unit test in each SDK with a fixed literal. Each golden was blessed from TypeScript alone, its diff read (only additions: two observations appended to `schedules-operate`, four `resetFrom` objects in `reset`, no line changed or removed), and the other five languages then reproduced it equal without bless (the whole suite, 76 scenarios in six languages, and `reset` and `schedules-operate` once more each).
- **D2147** (2026-11-10, review of the provenance slice, `contracts/journal-and-events.md`) **The provenance of a run lives on the run record, not in the journal detail.** The contract declared an optional `schedule {schedule_id, nominal_at_us, kind, revision}` on the `run.started` detail; nothing writes it (`RunStarted { entrypoint }`), and the REST `schedule` is read from the four columns of `runs`, which `verify_run` (replay equals projection) does not reproduce. Decided: it stays that way, and the contract line says so. A reset copies the journal verbatim and the new run's provenance is not the source's; a successor of a chain copies no schedule column (D2144), so a journal copy would claim what the record does not; the record is immutable (a trigger refuses a change) and written in the creation transaction with the action row, which is the authority. Replay equality of the four columns is **not claimed**. What it would cost, if it matters: G2068.
- **D2148** (2026-11-10, review) **A rule of `scripts/check-architecture.py` fails when a `run.finished` body is built without `add_schedule_origin`.** The shape of the message is four hand-copied `json!` literals, each patched by one call, so nothing failed for a fifth writer or an edit that dropped the call. `run_finished_errors` reads the adapter's source (the literals and comments masked, as the lease-row rule does) and requires `add_schedule_origin(` before every `"type": "run.finished"` body in the same function, that the function is defined, and that at least one body is found (a rule that checks nothing is an error). Nine tests in `scripts/tests/test_check_architecture.py` (a writer that calls it, one that does not and is named by file and line, a fifth writer, a call after the body, a call in another function, comments and strings, the SQL name of the event, no writer, no function). Hand mutant: the call removed from `loop_progress.rs`, the check fails naming `loop_progress.rs:969`; restored, it passes. The alternative (one function that builds the whole body) was left alone: the four writers differ in what they hold (a transaction, a command, a transition) and the shape is read by the AsyncAPI conformance test.
- **D2149** (2026-11-10, review, FR-018-046) **The chain test that stamps its head is named for what it is, and a test of the real behaviour sits next to it.** `chain_progress.rs` marks the head with `mark_as_scheduled` (the guard trigger of the columns off for one statement): it proves the finalization writer carries the data, so it is called `the_finalization_writer_says_the_schedule_a_stamped_head_carries_…` and says SYNTHETIC. The new `chain_handoff.rs` test `the_final_head_of_a_chain_a_schedule_started_has_no_schedule_and_its_webhook_says_none` runs a real chain: a real firing starts the first segment (it has `runs.schedule_id`), the scheduler's handoff creates the successor (none), the final head's `run.finished` has no `scheduleId`. The loop and collection tests say they stamp runs by hand too. The open item against FR-018-046 is G2063, visible in the contract and in `verification.md` (h).
- **D2150** (2026-11-10, review) **The business-id canary goes through the branch that adds properties, and the real-firing tests no longer depend on the wall clock.** The webhook test put the canary on a plain run only, so a `businessId` added inside `if let Some((Some(schedule), Some(nominal)))` would have been in no body and the exact-`data` assertion edited to match; a run that has both a business identifier and (stamped) a schedule now goes through that branch, its `data` is asserted key for key, and the loop that searches every body and every `event_outbox.payload` for both canaries stays. Hand mutant: `r.business_id` read and added in that branch: the test fails with the canary in the body; restored. `fire_at` now fires under a clock fixed five seconds after the nominal time (a test that straddled the next hour would have found the firing acting on that one too, or only on the latest under catch-up `ONE`), the comparison of the lengths of two strings is gone, and the comments say what is proved: the nominal time is passed through to the run; the schedule's own calculations shown are its first time (on the hour, after its creation) and the next one after the firing.
- **D2151** (2026-11-10, review, FR-018-047) **The Studio asks about a schedule only for a session that may read schedules, and shows a kind it does not know as written.** The hooks that ask for the name (`useNamespaceId`, the describe call) moved into `NamedSchedule`, rendered only with `schedule:read`, so a viewer without it triggers neither the describe call nor the list of namespaces (test: no call to either); the comment now says what is true: the link is always shown to a session that may read schedules, also for a schedule that was deleted, which is then called by the end of its id and whose page says it was not found (test kept and named). A kind this Studio does not know is shown clipped at 40 characters as written, no longer "another kind of action" (test); `sagat workflow inspect` clips the same way (test) and says `1 event` for a reset that keeps one (test). Hand mutants: the hooks unconditional, and the old words for an unknown kind: each fails its test; restored.
- **D2152** (2026-11-10, review) **`Run.schedule.kind` stays a closed enum, and the server does not tolerate a kind it does not know.** The column's check admits three values, so a fourth is a migration first; the contract notes (`journal-and-events.md`, the draft `rest.openapi.yaml`, the `kind` description in `openapi.yaml`) say that adding one is a compatibility decision of its own (the check, the enum, the SDKs, the Studio words). `run_schedule` turns an unreadable kind into a stored-data error, which would fail a whole `list` page in a mixed-version fleet after such a migration: not made tolerant (a two-line `None` would hide data from the very read that exists to show it, and no test of a fourth kind can be written before its migration exists): G2067.
- **D2153** (2026-11-10, review) **`add_schedule_origin` keeps its read for every finished run, subscriber or not.** `fan_out` returns early when no subscription wants the event, but the body is built before it; making the read conditional means asking whether a subscription exists first, which is a read of its own. The cost is stated in D2143 (one primary-key read of the row the transaction holds, per run finished): G2071.
- **D2154** (2026-11-10, review) **The doc of `selected()` says what it tolerates.** It tolerates the rows a command transaction reads (they do not select the columns); `get`, `list`, the graph read and `check_database_replay` name the columns and fail on a schema below `20261230000200`, as `business_id` and `start_delayed_until` already do. Only the engine's reads and `add_schedule_origin` tolerate it. No code change: G2070.
- **D2155** (2026-11-10, review) **The scan of the manifests for the reset switch has a test of its own, and the documents' counts are recomputed.** `no_shipped_manifest_turns_the_reset_switch_on` was the only test that read the real `infra/`; the function was tested, the scan was not, so a `value_given` that stopped reading the default of `${X:-true}` would have passed. The scan is a function now and `the_scan_finds_a_manifest_that_defaults_the_reset_switch_on_and_passes_one_that_defaults_it_off` feeds it a directory of its own (hand mutant: the default ignored, both that test and the function test fail; the real-manifest test alone stays green, which is why the new one exists). In `verification.md` the counts of the demo are those of a `grep` of the table (53 = 32 + 12 + 3 + 0 + 6), the rows SC-018-013, SC-018-015 and FR-018-049 agree with them, and the quickstart says the answer it shows is illustrative.
- **D2156** (2026-11-10, T018-055, SC-018-011, G1980 (1)) **The entry of a collection is a scoped event: the finish of a collection is never a reset point in this version.** Found by the corpus of the reset (D2157), seen red first. A collection of no item journals `collection.entered` and `collection.finished` at the root and nothing below it, so `reset_points` offered the finish (a `COLLECTION_FINISHED` point), the only point it could ever offer for a collection; the run made from it replayed to a state that holds the finished collection (`collections: {…}`) while the projection `restore_prefix` writes holds none (the `iteration_scopes` row is not re-created, which is what G1980 (1) says: "restoring a prefix with a scoped event needs the side tables of those scopes and none exists yet"), so `verify_run` failed: "projection differs from the journal". The test of the domain that said otherwise (`a_collection_is_a_point_when_it_has_finished_and_never_while_it_is_open`) held a journal no real run has, a collection whose iteration left no row below the root. **Fix:** `reset_points` sets `scoped` at `CollectionEntered` as it does for a row below the root, so every point after the entry of a collection is `ResetBlock::ScopedEvents` (`RESET_POINT_NOT_ELIGIBLE`); the conservative reading of G1980 (1), and the alternative (re-create the scope's rows, `REMATERIALIZE` of D1605) is the thing to build with the scoped events, not a patch of this one. A loop needs no clause: the finish of a loop that succeeds is no candidate (G1979 (1)) and a loop that does not succeed is no quiet state (`loop-empty`, a `while` that does not run, is in the corpus and offers the start only). **Tests:** `crates/domain/tests/reset.rs` (`a_collection_is_a_candidate_when_it_has_finished_but_its_scope_cannot_be_restored_yet`, the old test changed to say what a real journal says; `a_collection_with_no_item_holds_its_scope_all_the_same_so_its_finish_is_not_a_point`, new; both seen failing with the finish offered), `reset_corpus::a_collection_of_no_item_is_reset_only_at_its_start_as_one_of_two_is` (seen failing first with `projection differs from the journal`). The contract table (`contracts/journal-and-events.md`, the `collection.*` row) says what is built.
- **D2157** (2026-11-10, T018-055, SC-018-011) **The corpus of the reset is twelve runs of the real engine, each reset to every point `reset_points` offers, against a rule written by hand.** `tests/integration/schedules-bulk-operations/reset_corpus.rs`. The shapes, each ended by a real worker and a real scheduler (a collection and a loop need a scheduler that holds the shards and a worker of protocol 1.5 on a store that enables the scoped protocol, which the test builds): a parallel fork, a saga that completes, a saga that fails and is compensated, a collection of two items and one of none, a loop that runs and one that does not, a run with a child, a timer, a wait satisfied by a signal and one satisfied by a published event, and (over the REST routes, through the harness of the approvals) an approval that is decided. **The oracle is D1022 read by hand**, not `reset_points` read back: for each shape the points it must offer (kind and node) and, for each candidate it does not, why (`not-running:Waiting`, `open-work:mid`, `excluded:saga.aborting`, `scoped-events`), written before the journals were looked at and corrected only where the rule said the guess was wrong. What it says of the code, as built: the points are the start and the completion of an activity or a child at the root (`parallel`: start, `pre`, `post`; the first branch to end is `open-work`, the second is `not-running:Waiting`; `saga`: start, `pre`, `post`, the steps inside it `not-running:Waiting`; the compensation of an aborted saga `excluded:saga.aborting`; the collection and loop shapes: the start only (D2156); `child`: start, `pre`, `child.finished`, `post`; timer, wait and event: start, `pre`, `post`; approval: start and the activity before it). **The completion of a parallel (`parallel.joined`), of a saga (`saga.completed`), of a timer (`timer.fired`), of a wait (`wait.satisfied`), of an approval (`approval.decided`) and of a loop are no points**, as G1979 (1) says of loops and approvals and not only for them (G2072). For every point: the new run's projection equals a replay of its journal before it goes on and after it ends; rows `1..N` are the source's in every column but the two marks the reset adds (type, node, attempt, path, detail, payload digest, actor, trace, time) and each is marked `restored`, the next is `run.reset`, and nothing after it is marked; the root steps the source completed up to `N` hold the same status and output digest and the ones after it hold no output; the steps a reset kept are never leased again, and what a worker is given of them is what the source's worker was given (each execution gives an output of its own, so a step that ran again is told from one that was kept); the steps the source completed after `N` complete again, the children the prefix does not hold are started and the ones it holds are not; the run ends `SUCCEEDED`; every other sequence of the journal, and the one after its end, is `RESET_POINT_NOT_ELIGIBLE` for the reset and for the preview and makes no run; the register holds one entry per reset; the source's journal is what it was. **Hand mutants:** the open-work block of `state_block` removed (the parallel shape offers the branch: red), the `ExcludedEvent` block removed (the compensated saga's `reserve~c` is `not-running:Compensating` instead: red), the fix of D2156 removed (red). **Not in the corpus, said:** a declared state at each point (`the_declared_state_and_the_output_of_a_signal_in_the_prefix_are_restored` has one point), the 022 permit holder and the 020 attribute rows (features not approved), `RUN_PURGED` and test-tree refusals (the same), a prefix at the ceiling, and the branch of `RESET_NOT_SUPPORTED_FOR_SOURCE` for a run of a database execution: a trigger ties `origin` to an internal workflow, so no SQL makes one and none was forced (G1980 (5) stays as it is). The harness found nothing else wrong.
- **D2158** (2026-11-10, T018-046, SC-018-009) **One job of 1,000 real runs goes through four deaths of its process and a cancellation.** `tests/fault/schedules-bulk-operations/batch_thousand.rs`, both roles. 999 runs are started through the run service, eight at a time, and the thousandth is **started with a lock held on `telemetry_pending`** (taken, on a connection of its own, before it, after the switch of the crash and the monitor of the row locks were installed: installing a trigger waits for every lock on the table, which stalled the first version of this test), so its transaction has written its run, has an identity and is open when the job is created. The job cancels every run of the namespace at the highest rate (200 items a second) under the default executor and pass settings (a page of 500). Two replicas, each a keeper and a `BatchPass` in a task of its own over the pool the suite says (the least-privilege role when `SAGAT_TEST_AS_RUNTIME_ROLE=1`), hold the 64 shards between them. **While the transaction is open** five rounds of each replica take no step, a direct step of the executor is `Idle`, and the job is `SELECTING` with nothing selected; once it ends the job selects **1,000**, the held run among them. One watched round learns the writes of the transaction that records an item: three (`batch_items`, `batch_jobs`, `quota_transaction_deltas`). Then three times (after 100, 250 and 400 successes) the switch is armed on the first or the second of them: the owner of the job applies an item, **commits its effect and has its connection cut at the record**, the test waits until an item is `PENDING` whose run is `CANCELLED` (the effect without its record, read from the database), the owner's task is aborted, its leases lapse and its membership is deleted, a new process starts from nothing but the database, and a live one takes the shard and records the item. A fourth process is killed after 600 successes at a moment nothing chose. The first dead process, which still believes it holds its shards, is refused where it records (no step, none failed). After 700 the API cancels the job; the replicas drain it. **At the end, read from the database:** 4 deaths; `selected` 1,000 equals the items and the runs; 701 succeeded and 299 cancelled in the three runs as the owner (700 and 300 as the runtime role; the test asks for at least 700 and the rest cancelled), none skipped, failed or deferred, so the counters add up and no item is stuck; no run has two requests to stop it in its journal and no cancellation is audited twice; each item that succeeded has a run that is `CANCELLED` with one request, each item that was cancelled with the job has a run that is still `RUNNING` with none (the job undid nothing: `CANCELLED` runs equal `succeeded`); one record of the end of the job; the monitor of `pgrowlocks('runs')` looked 4,290 times and never saw a transaction with two run locks (the most one held was 1); no deadlock. **Nothing was found.** **Hand mutants:** a fresh key at each attempt (`batch:{job}:{run}:{uuid}`) is red with four items `SKIPPED` (a repeat after a death answers as the first only because the key is the same: the doubled-request assertions alone would not have seen it, because the state of a cancelled run refuses a second cancel), and a selection that does not wait for the horizon is red at the first round, which meets the held lock (the stage ends in a panic that names it after 120 s: a round of a job that must wait returns at once, which is the one wait of this test with a bound that is not minutes). Not run as a mutant: the refusal of the stale replica. **No broker hint exists to lose:** a job is found by the poll of the pass (`BatchPass::round`, `due_jobs`) and no message announces it, so the clause of the criterion has nothing to test (said in the row). The two keyed transactions (the effect and the record) are still what they were (G1961, O1951): the window between the look before the effect and the effect itself is not cut by a test.
- **D2159** (2026-11-10, T018-020, SC-018-002, G1018) **The steps of the firing that start no run are cut at each of their writes: twelve cases, nothing found.** `tests/fault/schedules-bulk-operations/firing_decisions.rs`, both roles. Each case builds a schedule in the state in which the next step is the one under test, watches that step on two schedules in the same state (the writing statements must be the same, in the same tables: a count that was not stable would be the finding), cuts the connection after each of its writes in turn on a schedule of its own, and asserts: the step answers an error; **no table of the database changed** (`all_table_digests`, every table including `schedules`); the schedule, its actions, its audit records and its backfill read as they did; the same step taken again at the same instant acts and leaves the state the watched step left (outcomes, times, counters, recovery, retry and recheck, audit records); no time has two action rows; the runs the schedule started replay to their projection; and a step armed past its last write is not cut. The cases and their writing statements: a time buffered (3), the look again at a waiting time (2), a time dropped with the buffer full (3), a recovery with no action left that coalesces what is owed (3; the state is made by writing `recovery_until` and `recovery_acts = 0`, the planner is the one that takes the step), times older than the window summarized as missed (3), a schedule with no action left (2), an ended run forgotten (2), a failed run forgotten and the schedule paused with its audit record (7), and the four steps of a backfill: a time skipped (4), buffered (4), found acted on already (3) and the end of the range (3). None of them makes a second transaction (a step is one transaction in each), and **the only audit record any of them writes is the one of the auto-pause, in the same transaction**. **Hand mutant:** `reconcile_schedule` committed before the audit record and the audit written in a second transaction: red at the first assertion (a step is one transaction). Two things the harness taught: the helper `Fixture::end` writes the status of a run into the table without a journal, so `verify_run` on a run it ended can only fail (the cases that need an ended run have a worker end it); and a namespace has room for four backfills at once (the matrix stops each one it made). The futures of these cases are boxed (a test thread has two megabytes and the first version overflowed it).
- **D2160** (2026-11-10, T018-013, SC-018-001) **The same instants in three processes: 440 cases, 13,200 instants, nothing found, and an independent oracle for the south.** `tests/integration/schedules-bulk-operations/calendar_processes.rs`. Eleven zones (Lisbon, New York, Sydney, Auckland, Santiago, Adelaide, Lord Howe with its half hour of daylight saving, Kolkata, Kathmandu with its three quarters, Newfoundland, UTC) by ten specs (a calendar at 02:30 with the gap skipped, at 02:30 shifted with jitter, at 01:30 taken twice, weekdays at 09:00 with jitter, the first and the fifteenth, every ninety minutes with an offset and jitter, hourly with jitter, two cron strings, weekdays but Mondays) by four dates (the months after 7 and 28 March and 20 September and 24 October 2026, which reach the changes of the north and of the south), each asking for the next thirty `(nominal, actual)` pairs of `upcoming` with a schedule id of its own. This process computes them; two others, started as this test binary (`--ignored --exact`, so that nothing but the child runs) with an empty environment and told opposite things (`TZ=UTC`, `LC_ALL=C`, `TZDIR=/nonexistent` and the root as the directory; `TZ=Pacific/Kiritimati`, Turkish locale, `TZDIR=/usr`, another test thread count, another directory), print what they find and the test compares the instants as integers of milliseconds (jitter moves more than a thousand of them from their nominal times). **They are equal**: the calendar embeds `chrono-tz` and reads no file, variable or clock, `croner` the same, and the jitter is the SHA-256 of the schedule id and the nominal millisecond. A calculation that depended on a random seed would differ in two processes by construction, and one that read `TZ` differs here: **hand mutant** (the digest of the jitter takes `$TZ` too): red, naming the case. The table of `schedule_calendar` has an oracle for Lisbon and New York only; eleven gaps and folds of the south and the half hour are checked here against the instants Python's `zoneinfo` gives (the system's zone files, not the database `chrono-tz` embeds): Sydney, Auckland, Newfoundland, Adelaide and Lord Howe skipped or shifted to the first instant after a gap of one hour or of thirty minutes, and taken once or twice in a fold, and the half-hour and three-quarter zones without daylight saving. **What this cannot test** is two processes **of two builds**: the zone database is the one the build embedded (G2073).

- **D2171** (2026-11-10, T018-016, FR-018-016) **The id of a scheduled run is `<prefix>-<nominal time>`, the time in RFC 3339 UTC to the whole second, ending in `Z`.** `application/src/schedule_firing.rs` (`nominal_text`, one function for the declared input field and for the id, so they cannot disagree). The seconds are whole because PostgreSQL keeps an instant to the microsecond and a Linux clock reads nanoseconds (a macOS one microseconds): an id that carried the fraction would depend on which clock read the instant, and a retried tick must make the same id. *Run now* takes the second of the request (two requests in one second under `FAIL` meet: the second is recorded `BUSINESS_ID_CONFLICT`, tested); a backfill takes the nominal time it acts on; a schedule a cron trigger owns has no prefix and its run is never bound. A prefix of 160 characters, a dash and 20 characters of time make 181 of the 200 an id may have, so an id the domain refuses is not reachable (`for_nominal_time` still refuses rather than truncates). The window of the binding is the default one (30 days), as for every other start (G2085).
- **D2172** (2026-11-10, T018-016, FR-018-016, ADR-0050 decision 7) **The claim is the person's claim: nothing of the semantics was forked.** `RunService::start_scheduled_under` passes the `BusinessStart` (id, the two policies, the window) through the same `start_with` and `NewRun.business` that a start uses, so `business_ids::claim` runs in `PgRunStore::create` after `lock_for_admission` (the fence and the schedule's row) and before the run's locks: the plan's row 2 (`schedule row → business-id row → admission`). `slot_order.rs` gained the case (`a_scheduled_action_under_a_business_id_takes_the_schedule_then_the_binding_…`, a start at every lock of the reference: green) and a competing claim that has not committed makes the firing wait for it in the claim (`a_firing_that_waits_for_a_competing_claim_…`, held with an uncommitted insert and `pg_stat_activity`, no sleep). The idempotency fingerprint of the run is the action's own (`schedule:{id}`, `nominal-{ms}`) and does not take the id: the time is the identity, and a replay of it is `Replayed` before the claim, so a retried tick never claims twice (`a_time_that_is_acted_on_again_claims_nothing_twice`).
- **D2173** (2026-11-10, T018-016, FR-018-016, FR-018-019, FR-018-020) **A held id is the decision of the time: recorded, terminal, never retried.** `FAIL` with an open holder: the outcome `BUSINESS_ID_CONFLICT` (in the closed set since `20261230000000`) with the reason `BUSINESS_ID_IN_USE`, no run. A closed holder that `REJECT_DUPLICATE` (or `ALLOW_DUPLICATE_FAILED_ONLY` after a success) refuses: `BUSINESS_ID_CONFLICT` with the reason `BUSINESS_ID_REUSE_REJECTED` (added to the closed list of reasons of an action; the column takes any `[A-Z][A-Z0-9_]{0,63}`, so no migration), no run. `USE_EXISTING` with an open holder: `ALREADY_ACTED`, reason `BUSINESS_ID_IN_USE`, `runId` the run that answers (the current segment of a chain, as `startRun` answers), nothing started. **Why terminal and not "owed and retried"** (the rule for an admission the platform refuses for lack of room): a quota comes back, a held id is the answer of a policy the person chose, and a later retry would claim the id of a time that is already past; `SKIPPED_OVERLAP` is the same kind of decision. So no `retry_at`, no `ADMISSION_RETRY`, `remainingActions` is **not** used up (it is decremented only by an action that starts a run, FR-018-012), no run is tracked, the schedule goes on to the next time and **does not pause** (`pauseOnFailure` reacts to a run of the schedule that fails; a refused action has none). The same for a backfill step (its counter takes the outcome and its cursor moves) and for trigger-now (the `202` carries the decision, a retry of the key the same one). The decision of a backfill or trigger-now commits its audit (`schedule.triggered`) and key with it.
- **D2174** (2026-11-10, T018-016) **The decision is recorded after the claim's transaction ended, not inside it.** `create` rolls the claim back (so the binding's row is not held while the connection waits to be reused) and the firing then commits the decision under the same fence and the same compare-and-swap on the schedule's state. A crash between the two leaves nothing and the next pass decides again; a holder that closed in the round trip between is a decision the policy took at the instant it looked, as a stale read of the open runs only delays (D1005). Recording inside the creation transaction was considered and refused: it needs a new answer of `RunStore::create` carried through every caller (reset, batch, with-start, the event pass) for one path, to close a window the policy tolerates. `fault … firing::`: the decision's transaction is cut at each of its four writes (`held-id`), and the start under an id at each of the 34 writes of the transaction that also writes the binding (`start-with-a-prefix`, against the 28 of a start without one): nothing changes, and the retry makes one run and one binding, or the one decision.
- **D2175** (2026-11-10, T018-016) **A waiting action that finds its id held is settled where it stands.** An action that waited for a run to end (`BUFFER_*`, `CANCEL_OTHER`) claims its id when it is released. `record_decision` settles the `BUFFERED` row (the one outcome an action leaves, which the guard of the table lets go to a final one) as the decision (`settle_waiting`, only for `BUSINESS_ID_CONFLICT` and `ALREADY_ACTED`), counts one waiting action less and leaves `remainingActions` (the reserved one comes back). One row, never two (`a_waiting_action_that_finds_its_id_held_is_settled_and_never_started`).
- **D2176** (2026-11-10, T018-016, FR-018-043) **Where a refused action names its field, and what it refuses.** A target the domain refuses now says which field (`/action/businessIdPrefix`, `/action/nominalTimeField`, `/action/runTimeoutMs`) and not `/action`; the reason is `SCHEDULE_SPEC_INVALID` (a prefix belongs to the schedule's action, not to the options of a start, so `BUSINESS_ID_INVALID` is not used and no reason is added), and the refusal never echoes the text (`rest::a_prefix_that_is_not_one_is_refused_with_its_place_and_never_echoed`). A policy with no prefix is refused (`/action/businessIdConflictPolicy`, `/action/businessIdReusePolicy`) instead of silently dropped, as a start refuses a policy without an id (D1965). `422 business identifiers on schedules are not available yet` and the `InvalidStart` `BUSINESS_ID_NOT_APPLICABLE` of the run service are gone.
- **D2177** (2026-11-10, G2027, FR-018-022, FR-018-027) **A batch selector by `businessIdPrefix` matches runs through a range of the index that exists.** `infra-postgres/src/batch.rs` (`matching_runs_by_prefix!`, `SELECTION_BY_PREFIX_SQL`, `PREVIEW_BY_PREFIX_SQL`): `r.business_id ~>=~ prefix AND r.business_id ~<~ end`, `end` being the prefix with its last character one step up (`~` becomes DEL, which no id holds): the operators of `text_pattern_ops`, so the range is an index condition of `runs_business_id_idx (tenant_id, namespace_id, business_id text_pattern_ops) WHERE business_id IS NOT NULL` (migration `20270103000000`, so **no migration here**) **whatever plan the statement gets** (the proof asks the planner for a generic plan and, on a namespace of 6,001 runs with statistics, shows the index in the plan of both statements), and a literal match: `%`, `_` and `\` match themselves (`LIKE` was the other way and a parameter of `LIKE` is not an index condition in a generic plan). A selector with a prefix has its own statement text, so that the plan a database caches for the many jobs without one is never the plan of one with. It matches the **runs** (`runs.business_id`, the copy the run carries), not the bindings: a run whose binding was purged after its window is still selected (tested), a run whose id feature 020 erased (the column is null) is not; it stays inside the tenant and the namespace of the job (tested with a run of another namespace and one of another tenant); it composes with the other filters; a prefix that matches nothing counts zero and a job over it completes with no item (tested, and over REST). The page is by run id, as the other filters, so a prefix that matches most of the namespace reads and sorts most of it each page, which the runbook says.
- **D2178** (2026-11-10, G2027) **`BATCH_SELECTOR_UNSUPPORTED` stays in the closed set and has no producer.** The set of reasons grows only and the SDKs treat an unknown value as another one; the `020` query selector is `QUERY_UNSUPPORTED_OPERATOR` (feature 020's reason). The remediation sentence, which said "Business ids are not bound to runs yet", is now "This deployment cannot evaluate that selector…" (`api-http/src/error.rs`, `contracts/errors.md`, the runbooks).
- **D2179** (2026-11-10, T018-016, FR-018-047) **The six SDKs, the CLI and the Studio.** Typed optional `businessIdPrefix`, `businessIdConflictPolicy`, `businessIdReusePolicy` on the schedule action's write and read types of every SDK, the policies of the write the types `start` has, on a read the string as sent; absent means absent on the write and on the observation (the conformance runners print the three keys only when present, so no existing golden moved); the prefix sent as given and kept out of the debug text of the types that hold it. The batch selector's prefix was typed in all six already. The scenarios `schedules-operate` (a schedule with a prefix, its defaults, two refusals, a trigger-now and the run it made) and `batch-operations` (runs under a prefix, a preview that counts them, zero, a wildcard, a malformed prefix, a job that cancels exactly those). The CLI: `schedule create|update --business-id-prefix [--if-exists] [--reuse]` merged over the file's action, `batch preview|create --business-id-prefix` joined to the filter (or alone), `schedule get` and `actions` say the prefix and the reasons. The Studio: the prefix and the two policies on the schedule form with the id it would make, the page, the decisions with their reasons in words, and the prefix filter in the batch dialog.
- **D2180** (2026-11-10, FR-018-044) **No new audit action for a held id.** A decision that starts no run (a skip, a buffer, a drop) writes the action row and no audit record, and the decision of a held id does the same: the row (kind, nominal time, outcome, reason) is the durable record, counted by `schedule_actions_total{kind,outcome}`. The audit of a manual request (`schedule.triggered`) still commits with its decision. FR-018-044 reads as if business-id conflicts of a person's start were audited too; they are not (G2086). *Superseded 2026-11-13 by D2218: the owner chose an audit action (OD-2026-11-13-3); the action row stays the record of the decision, and the audit record now commits beside it.*
- **D2181** (2026-11-10, FR-018-015, ADR-0050 decision 7) **A decision about a held id is made under the authority the schedule has now, as a run is.** Found by the independent security review of this slice and shown by a test that failed first (`a_revocation_that_commits_after_the_action_read_the_membership_stops_an_action_whose_id_is_held_too`, red: `BUSINESS_ID_CONFLICT` where `AUTHORITY_REVOKED` is due). The claim ends the transaction (a holder returned, a conflict) before the place where a run's admission looks at the subject again, so a revocation that committed after the firing read the membership was recorded as a decision about the id (under `USE_EXISTING`, with the holder's run id) instead of `AUTHORITY_REVOKED`. `recheck_schedule_authority` (the catalog of a workflow with managed database steps, then the membership under its lock, the order of the admission) now runs in both arms before the rollback, and in the admission itself, from one function; the plain case (a revocation that committed before the firing read the membership) was already caught by the pass and is a test (`an_author_who_lost_the_permission_is_revoked_whatever_holds_the_id`). The reviewer's claim that a schedule goes on acting for a revoked author for as long as the id stays held was wrong (the pass reads the membership every step); the window was the race.
- **D2182** (2026-11-10, FR-018-012, D1042) **A waiting action settled because its id was held gives back the action it kept, and the schedule goes on.** Found by the independent correctness review and shown by a test that failed first (`a_waiting_action_that_finds_its_id_held_gives_back_the_action_it_kept_and_the_schedule_goes_on`, red: `next_action_at` stayed `NULL`).
