A waiting action keeps the last remaining action for itself (`decide` answers `Exhausted` while `remaining - buffered` is zero, and `Exhausted` clears the next times), so the schedule has none while it waits; settled without using the action up, the schedule was left `ACTIVE` with an action and no time to act on for ever. `record_held` recomputes the next times from now when the admission was a scheduled one, had none and the schedule has an action left (the time that fell due meanwhile was passed over without a decision, as `Exhausted` does; a schedule past its end has none either way). **Amended by D2190:** the give-back is for a waiting *scheduled* action; a waiting trigger-now or backfill step keeps no remaining action and needs none (the reservation counts scheduled rows only), which closed the same stranding for them.
- **D2183** (2026-11-10) **What the two reviews found that is not a defect, or is left.** `GREATEST(buffered + $6, 0)` of the first version is gone (the check `buffered >= 0` is the guard again, and `settle_waiting` counts one less only when a row was settled). `FilterBody` of the API prints no prefix in `Debug`. The CLI takes `--if-exists` and `--reuse` alone when the file names the prefix. *Run now* and a scheduled time of the same schedule share one id space per second (the runbook says so; D2171 shows it for two run-nows). That an `ALREADY_ACTED` names the run of whoever holds an id is acceptable because every role that reads schedules reads runs (`identity.rs`); a test that says so is not written (G2090). Predictable ids can be squatted by any `run:start` holder, who can already pause the schedule (G2089).

- **D2186** (2026-11-10, FR-018-014, FR-018-016; third review, correctness) **A waiting action is made under the schedule as it stands when it is released, so its prefix is the head revision's; its row keeps the revision it was decided under.** The reviewer asked which of the two rules the code follows. `fire` builds the admission of a released action with `waiting.revision` (which only the row keeps), and `start_run` reads `record.target`, which is the *head* revision's action (`select_schedules!` joins the revision `= head_revision`): the workflow, the version policy, the input, the nominal-time field, the timeout and, with this slice, the prefix and its two policies are the head's, as the policies of the schedule (overlap, buffer) always were. So the existing rule is the head's, and the prefix follows it instead of making a rule of its own. FR-018-014 ("an action uses the revision it was decided under") holds for the row, not for what the run is made of; it is not changed here. Shown by `scheduled_business_ids::a_waiting_action_takes_the_prefix_of_the_head_revision_when_it_is_released` (buffered under revision 1 with the prefix `old`, the schedule updated to revision 2 with `new`, released: the run is under `new-<nominal time>` and the row says revision 1). A **characterization** test of a rule that already held: no code changed, so it could not be seen red; it fails when the claim reads anything but the head's target. The runbook says so (section 1.1). Making the revision's own target the rule would need the store to read the target and the input digest of the revision a row was decided under: not built, and a new decision if the owner wants it.
- **D2187** (2026-11-10, FR-018-042; third review, security) **A hash of a request is not the text: the existing rule for every request fingerprint.** What each request keeps, checked: the creation of a batch job keeps a SHA-256 of its fingerprint in `idempotency` (`application/src/batch.rs`, the selector with its prefix as JSON); a person's start keeps one that covers its business id (`run.business` in `runs.rs`, hashed by `definitions::hash`); the create and the update of a schedule require the `Idempotency-Key` header and keep **no** fingerprint (a name in use and a stale revision answer a retry), so the action document with its prefix is not hashed anywhere. A SHA-256 of low-entropy text (a prefix, an id) can be guessed offline by whoever can read the `idempotency` table, as it can for every other request the platform fingerprints (an input, a name). The runtime role is the only reader and the table is in no response, log or event. Not changed: a keyed hash (an HMAC under a per-tenant key) would change every fingerprint of the platform at once and is its own decision (not registered as a gap: the existing rule).
- **D2188** (2026-11-10, FR-018-005, D2174; third review, architecture) **A held id is decided in a second transaction, and a holder that closes in between still yields the decision.** The claim is made in the creation transaction and rolled back when it finds the id held (so that the binding's row is not held while the connection waits); the decision is committed in a second transaction under the same shard fence and state-version compare-and-swap. A holder that closes between the two turns the time into a terminal decision that says "held" (`BUSINESS_ID_IN_USE`, true when the claim was made), where a person's start, retried by its caller, would then succeed. Accepted: the time is the schedule's own and is acted on once (retrying it would be a second action for one nominal time, which the identity of the action refuses); the decision names what was true when the id was asked for; and the alternative, to hold the binding across the second transaction, is the lock the order of ADR-0050 decision 7 exists to avoid. Written in the lock-order table of `plan.md` (rows 2 and 3), not in the wording of a requirement.
- **D2189** (2026-11-10, FR-018-005; third review, architecture) **"Every nominal time has one decision" has one exception, and the runbook says it.** The give-back of D2182 resumes a schedule from the first time after the settling and the time that fell due while a waiting *scheduled* action kept the last remaining action was passed over without a decision row (`Exhausted` records nothing: D1042). After D2190 a waiting trigger-now or backfill step keeps no action, so the exception is that case only. `docs/runbooks/schedules.md` section 1.1 names it instead of promising a decision for every time.
- **D2190** (2026-11-10, FR-018-012, D1042, G1017; third review, correctness; **a defect, half of it older than this slice**) **A waiting action keeps a remaining action for itself only when it will use one up, which only a scheduled action does.** *What was wrong:* `decide` answers `Exhausted` when `remaining - buffered` is zero, and `buffered` counted every waiting row whatever its kind, so a waiting trigger-now or backfill step (which `FR-018-012` and `trigger_once` say never use a remaining action up) made the next scheduled time "exhausted" and cleared the next times. The give-back of D2182 was guarded by `admission.kind == Scheduled`. So, with `remainingActions` left, a schedule with a waiting manual action was left `ACTIVE` with an action and no next time for ever, **whether the waiting action then started** (older than this slice: G1017 described it as "a time early" and missed that nothing ever gives the time back) **or found its business id held** (the settled case is the slice's, the same cause). Shown red by `waiting_reservation::` (five tests: a trigger-now and a backfill step, each started and settled, and the due time passed over): `(Active, Some(1), 0, None)` where `Some(14:00)` is due, in all four. *Fix, at the root:* `Standing` carries `reserved`, the waiting actions that use one up (`domain/src/schedules.rs`), counted by the store as the waiting rows of kind `SCHEDULED` (`waiting_scheduled`, read only when a limit is set and something waits); `decide` subtracts `reserved`, not `buffered` (the buffer limits and the idle test still count every waiting row). A time that falls due while a manual action waits is now decided like any other (`DROPPED_BUFFER_FULL` under `BUFFER_ONE`, a buffered row under `BUFFER_ALL`) instead of passed over, and no give-back is needed for a manual action; the give-back of D2182 stays for a waiting scheduled action. Hand mutants: the old reservation (every waiting row) is caught by all five, and counting every kind in the store likewise. G1017 closed; schedules that were left stranded before this change are not repaired by it (G2092).
- **D2191** (2026-11-10, G2027, G2089; third review, security) **The preview by a business-id prefix does not sort: it counts, up to the most it reads, and samples.** `PREVIEW_BY_PREFIX_SQL` had `ORDER BY r.id LIMIT n`; the range of the index of the business ids is ordered by business id, so the database read and sorted every run the prefix reaches to hand back the first few, whatever the limit (an `EXPLAIN` showed `Limit -> Sort -> Index Scan on runs_business_id_idx`). Without the order it takes the first matches and stops. Red first: the plan assertion of `a_selective_prefix_is_answered_from_the_index_on_the_business_id_of_the_runs` (the preview has no `Sort`), seen failing on the ordered statement; a second case, a wide prefix that nearly every run matches, asserts the plan stops at its limit with no sort (the planner then reads the table, a few hundred rows, not the index: both are bounded by the limit). The sample is therefore the first runs in the order of the index and not the lowest run ids; the contract says "a few of them" and no order (`rest.openapi.yaml`, `sample`), and the conformance runner sorts what it compares. The **selection** of a job cannot do the same: it pages by run id (the cursor is a run id) and keeps its `ORDER BY` (G2093).
- **D2192** (2026-11-10, G2089; third review, security) **How a failing selection round is handled, for every selector: it is retried at every poll, for ever; no bound fits without a migration, so none is built.** Read, not assumed: `select_round` sets a ten-second `statement_timeout`; a round that exceeds it fails with the store's error, `BatchExecutor::select` passes it on (`refused` turns only a `StateConflict` into a refusal), and the pass logs "a batch step failed", counts `round.failed` and tries again at the next poll (`scheduler/src/batches.rs`). `batch_jobs` has no failure counter and no last error; `failure_reason = 'SELECTION_FAILED'` exists in the schema, the domain (`FailureReason::SelectionFailed`) and the codec and **is raised nowhere**. The same holds for a selector with no prefix (a status filter over a very large namespace) and for a list of run ids. A durable bound needs a column (a count of consecutive failures, reset by a round that picks), which is a migration; a counter in the memory of the executor would be a bound that a restart or a move of the shard lease resets, so it is not one. Not built (the brief says not to add a migration); G2093 says what it costs and what the fix needs. **Superseded by D2197 (2026-11-11): the migration was written and the bound is built.**
- **D2193** (2026-11-10, D2174; third review, correctness) **The order "replay, then claim" in `create` is not observable, and the test that was thought to show it did not.** `a_time_that_is_acted_on_again_claims_nothing_twice` was asked to show that a time acted on twice does not claim its id first. With the claim first (a hand mutant: the replay skipped for a start with a business id) the test still passed: the claim then finds the first run as the holder, the firing records a decision, and the identity of the action's row (tenant, schedule, origin, nominal time) refuses it, so the answer is the same `Refused` and the state is the same. The two orders cannot be told apart from outside, in this path or in the others (a scheduled replay is `StateConflict`; a backfill step and a trigger-now check their action or their key before they get there). The test is renamed to what it proves (`a_time_that_is_acted_on_twice_leaves_one_run_one_binding_and_one_row`: one run, one binding whose holder does not change, one `STARTED` row, no decision of a conflict with itself, the counters) and says so. The order stays as it is: a replay answered before an id is claimed costs one transaction less.
- **D2194** (2026-11-10; third review, all three) **Small things done, and two left.** Done: `TargetParts` prints no prefix in `Debug` (`no_debug_text_of_a_target_holds_its_prefix`, red against `#[derive(Debug)]`); the `Debug` of `FilterBody` and of the selector that holds it is under a unit test of its own (red when it prints the prefix); every role that reads schedules also reads runs, as a unit test over the role table with an exhaustive `match` on the roles, so that D2183 stays true when a role changes (red when `viewer` loses `run:read`); `SELECTION_BY_PREFIX_SQL` and `PREVIEW_BY_PREFIX_SQL` are `#[doc(hidden)]` in the re-export; the CLI's length check of the prefix says in its comment that it is a convenience the platform repeats. A batch twin of the privacy canary test (`privacy::the_prefix_of_a_batch_selector_reaches_only_the_job_that_holds_it`: a preview, a job run to its end, the reads and a replay of the creation; every table of the database but `batch_jobs` (which holds the selector: customer text by its migration), `business_ids` and `runs` (the ids themselves), the logs, the spans and the metrics; red when the prefix is logged). The three statements that reach runs by prefix (the list, the preview, the items of a job) are compared on one corpus of ids at the edges of the range (`%`, `_`, `\`, `~`, `|`, `}`, a prefix that ends in or is made of them, ids just outside the range): `the_list_the_preview_and_the_job_reach_the_same_runs_for_a_prefix_at_every_edge`; a mutant that makes the range end of the **selection** statement alone inclusive is caught by it and by no other test. Left, as not trivial or not worth it: the clone of the admission on every firing (`decided = admission.clone()`: avoiding it changes the signature of `ScheduledStarts::start_scheduled` to hand the admission back on a refusal, for one small allocation per firing), and one source for the literal `BUSINESS_ID_IN_USE` (the store, the API, the reset path and the firing spell it; a shared constant touches four crates for a string).
- **D2195** (2026-11-10, FR-018-016; third review, correctness) **The Studio does not read spaces alone as "no prefix".** The form trimmed the prefix, so a prefix of spaces became an empty one and the schedule was written without a prefix or its policies, silently. An empty field still means no prefix; spaces alone are refused on the field ("Spaces alone are not a prefix: write one, or empty the field"), by one function (`businessPrefixProblem`) that the form's field and the request's check both use; the text around a prefix is still trimmed. Red first: `model.test.ts` (`is never dropped silently…`) and `ScheduleFormPage.test.tsx` (the prefix that is spaces alone), which replaced the two tests that asserted the silent drop.
- **D2196** (2026-11-10, T018-072) **The CI failure of `batch_pass::the_engines_loop_applies_the_items_of_a_lone_job_at_its_slots_and_not_at_its_poll` was the test's wait, not the platform.** It failed at its first stage ("the job selects its runs") in three of the four CI runs that ran it and passed in every local one (also beside eight busy loops). The diagnostic of `until_running` (`c8289d97`, `5426ea6c`) caught it on the fourth: at the 180 s bound the job was `COMPLETED`, its horizon (22165) below the oldest open transaction of the server (59495), so nothing held the selection back. The wait loops *rounds* until the job is `RUNNING`, and one round takes up to `steps_per_job` (20) steps: it ends the selection and goes on to apply every item whose slot has come. The four items of the job are 50 ms apart; on a loaded runner each step costs more than that, so all four are due by the time the first is applied and the round leaves the job `COMPLETED`, never seen `RUNNING`. A throwaway probe showed the mechanism at the highest rate (5 ms slots): one round, five steps, `Selecting` to `Completed` (removed again). The test now waits with `until_selection_over` (`RUNNING` or `COMPLETED`); its assertions (four selected, four succeeded, none failed) hold either way, and the claim about the slots is still measured where a step is faster than a slot. The other five callers of `until_running` use the slowest rate (a slot 1,000 s ahead) and cannot meet it. No platform change: the loop applies items at their slots, which is what the test asserts.
- **D2197** (2026-11-11, G2093, D2192, FR-018-029) **A selection round that fails is counted in the database and bounded: ten failed rounds in a row are survived, the eleventh ends the job `SELECTION_FAILED`.** The bound is the retries of an item (`DEFAULT_ITEM_RETRIES`, 10) with its wait (5 s doubling to 5 min, one function `backoff` for both, so they cannot drift apart): `sagat_domain::batch::selection_retry(failures)` is `After(5, 10, 20, 40, 80, 160, 300, 300, 300, 300 s)` for the first ten and `Exhausted` for the eleventh. It is a count and not a count and a window, which the item's retry has: the wait is what spaces the rounds (the store refuses a round before its time, D2198), so ten retries are at least 1,515 s apart and the window is implicit; no instant of a first failure has to be kept, which would have been a second column. Why ten and ~25 minutes: one timeout on a loaded database must not fail a job a person made (several in a row are needed, each after a longer wait), and a database that cannot answer one statement of one job for 25 minutes is an outage the person should hear about, as the job's end is; the cost of the wait is at most eleven statements of ten seconds (about 110 s of one connection in 25 minutes) for a selector that never succeeds. **The count must survive a restart and a move of the shard lease, which is why it is a column**: `batch_jobs.selection_failures smallint NOT NULL DEFAULT 0` (migration `20270116000000_batch_selection_failures`). The executor adds one in **one fenced transaction of its own** (`BatchStore::selection_failed`: lease fence, then the job row `FOR UPDATE`, the plan's row 14 order) when `select_round` fails (the failed round rolled back its own work), and a round that picks sets it to 0 **in its own transaction**, in the same `UPDATE` that moves the cursor and the state, so the count is only ever of failures in a row; at the eleventh, the same transaction ends the job. A round that gets as far as picking is progress whether or not it finishes the selection (a short page, or one at `maxItems`, moves the job to `RUNNING`; a page that picks is a page of the cursor). Red first, seen failing for the right reason with the record of the failure made the behaviour before (the error handed on, nothing recorded): the tests of `batch_selection_failures` (`step` answered the error, the count stayed 0, the job stayed `SELECTING`), see `verification.md` (k). **Why the store decides retry or end, and not the executor** (a review asked): the count, the wait and the end must be one transaction under one lock of the job row, and the decision is made on the count read under that lock; an executor that read the count, decided, and then asked the store to write would decide on a number that another step or a cancel can have moved (D2204 shows the cancel). So the port is `BatchStore::selection_failed`, its answer is a type with meaning of its own (`SelectionFailure`: the job waits, the job ended, it is not selecting any more), the policy stays the domain's (`selection_retry`, a pure function the store applies), and the port's doc says so. The executor's private step that calls it is `record_failed_round` (it was named as the port method). The executor builds the audit record of the end on every failure and the store writes it only when the job ends, which costs one small allocation for a round that failed (a job fails a round once in several seconds at the most): a closure for it would make the port generic over a closure and the wrapper stores of the tests with it, for nothing.
- **D2198** (2026-11-11, G2093, G2043) **The wait of a job whose round failed is its `next_slot_at`, and the store refuses a round before it.** `next_slot_at` is the durable slot (`the earliest instant the next step of the job may be taken`, in the data model for a running job's item, and now for a selecting job's round): the failure write sets it to the database's `clock_timestamp()` plus the backoff (`make_interval(secs => …)`, never the process's clock). The due-scan already reads `next_slot_at <= t0` as an index condition of `batch_jobs_due_idx (shard, next_slot_at)`, so a job that waits is **not read at all** (no change to the scan, its index or the hint), it leaves the head of the scan until its time and the jobs behind it are reached (the stuck-row starvation of this feature's reviews: a failing job that stays due would be the head of every round, for ever; `a_job_that_waits_leaves_the_scan_until_its_time_and_the_jobs_behind_it_are_reached` is that case, with a scan that takes one job a round), and at its time and after it the scan returns it (`due_jobs_as_of` at `slot - 1 ms`, `slot`, `slot + 1 ms`). `request_cancel` already pulls the slot back (`LEAST(next_slot_at, at)`), so a waiting job is cancelled at once. **A step decided before the failure was recorded does not run the round again**: `select_round` reads `clock_timestamp() >= next_slot_at` with the job row and answers `Selected::Waiting` when it has not come (a replica that took the job over on a stale scan would otherwise count two failures where there was one wait). `Stepped::Backoff` is the executor's answer to a failure that was recorded: the pass counts it as a failed step and leaves the job (`round.failed`, no hint), so the loop does not spin (the job is not due), and the job is found again within one poll after its time (the hint `next_slot_in` is of running jobs only, G2095). The lag gauge of the monitor (`scheduler_lag_seconds{pass="batches"}`, `infra-postgres/src/operations.rs`) reads the same `next_slot_at <= now()`, so a job that waits is not late; one whose time has come and is not stepped is, as any due job.
- **D2199** (2026-11-11, G2093) **A job that fails its selection ends by the writes `AUTHORITY_REVOKED` uses, and keeps what it had.** `fail_job` and the failure past the bound share one function (`end_failed`): the items that had not ended become `CANCELLED` (so the counters equal the outcomes of the items), the job takes `state = FAILED`, `failure_reason = 'SELECTION_FAILED'`, `finished_at`, and the audit record `batch.finished` with the reason is written, by the engine as the actor (the executor's `finished`), in the same transaction as the last failure is counted (the count that is left, 11, is the evidence of how many rounds failed). A job fails its selection only while `SELECTING`, so it has **no item applied** (items are applied only while `RUNNING`): what it keeps are the items its earlier pages selected, which become `CANCELLED` with their runs untouched (`a_pick_that_does_not_finish_the_selection_…` asserts the two items cancelled, `selected = cancelled = 2`, and no run `CANCELLED`); a job that fails by `AUTHORITY_REVOKED` while `RUNNING` keeps its applied items the same way (`SUCCEEDED` stays). There is no event table in this path: the audit record is the record, as for `AUTHORITY_REVOKED`. A `CANCELLING` job is never failed (the cancel wins, the state machine's rule): a failure recorded for a job that is no longer `SELECTING` is `SelectionFailure::NotSelecting` and records nothing.
- **D2200** (2026-11-11, G2093) **What counts as a failed round.** Every `StoreError` of `select_round` except `StateConflict`: a statement over its ten-second timeout, a deadlock victim or serialisation failure, a database error that comes again, a pool or connection error that the record then survives. `StateConflict` (the lease moved or the job did) is `Stepped::Refused`, as before, and is not a failure of the selection; `Selected::Waiting` (a transaction of the creation is still open, or a backoff has not passed) is not one either. A connection that the database or the network cut in the middle of the round's transaction is such a failure too (the process lives on and records it; the fault matrix of the selection page in `batch.rs` therefore answers `Backoff` at the positions where the record's own transaction is not cut, and asserts that no page committed and the failure is on record once). A database that is down fails the recording too, so its outage does not spend the bound: nothing is recorded, the round is tried again at the next poll, as before. The error is **not an argument** of `selection_failed` and its text is not read: it can quote a value of the selector (the SQLSTATE is, D2203). **Every error but a `StateConflict` counts, `Unavailable` included (pool exhaustion, a failover, timeouts that the load of another tenant's work causes), and that has a consequence to say out loud**: in a platform-wide incident in which the database still answers the small statement that records a failure but fails the rounds for about 25 minutes, every selecting job ends `FAILED` with its pending items `CANCELLED`. That is irreversible (there is no resume; the runs it selected are untouched; the person creates the job again), and it is the cost of a bound that does not ask why a round failed. The bound is a decision about how long the platform waits for a selection (O1953), not a statement about the selector; the runbook says so (a burst of `outcome="failed"` across many jobs and tenants is the database, not the selectors), and G2100 records that no job can be retried.
- **D2201** (2026-11-11, G2093) **What the migration holds.** `selection_failures smallint NOT NULL DEFAULT 0` (a constant default: a catalog change, no rewrite; an older engine never writes it and an older reader never selects it), `CHECK (selection_failures >= 0)`, and **no** rule that ties the count to the state of the job. A first version had one (`selection_failures = 0 OR state IN ('SELECTING','CANCELLING','CANCELLED','FAILED')`, so that a pick that did not reset the count would be refused by the table); it was dropped before this was written down because it breaks Constitution IV: an older engine that takes the shard of a job that waits picks its page without knowing the column, and the CHECK would refuse its write and stop it. A running job with a stale count is harmless (nothing reads the count once the selection is over), and that a pick of this build resets it is held by tests and a hand mutant, not by the table (`batch_selection_failures_migration` asserts the older engine's pick is not refused). The guard trigger `batch_jobs_guard` lists what a job is and never changes; the new column is progress like the counters and **needs no change**; that only the executor under the fence of the shard writes it is the store's `check_fence` in the same transaction, which a trigger cannot see, and the runtime role writes it with the table-wide `UPDATE` it holds (no grant moves; tested as `sagat_app`). A smallint is not variable-size: nothing is classified in `storage-classification.json`. **The `down` refuses while a job that is still selecting holds a count above zero** (`object_not_in_prerequisite_state`: the job would lose its progress to the bound; **this was "any job" in the first version and is narrowed by D2209**: a job that ended `SELECTION_FAILED` keeps 11 as evidence and its reason says the rest); it restores the schema otherwise (the column goes, every job stays), after which the `up` applies again (`batch_selection_failures_migration`). An older engine that picks a page does not reset the count (mixed versions: a job can fail up to ten rounds early after a rolling deploy; accepted, D2209). ROLLOUT ORDER: apply the migration before the engine that counts. An engine that runs against a schema without the column cannot select at all (the statement of a round that picks writes the count, and the record of a failure writes it too): every round fails, none can be recorded, and the jobs wait, as in an outage, until the migration is applied; no job is ended by it (the only end is the record of the eleventh failure, which needs the column). An older engine against the new schema is not affected: it never writes the column and reads none.
- **D2202** (2026-11-11, G2093) **What is observed, and what is not.** `batch_selection_failures_total{outcome}` is counted once the record is committed, with two closed values: `retry` (the round was counted and the job waits) and `failed` (the job ended `SELECTION_FAILED`); no tenant, job, selector, error or reason label (the same closure the other batch series have; `a_selection_that_always_fails_…` reads the page and asserts the two labels and the two values). The log line has the id of the job and the count and nothing else (`a batch selection round failed; the job waits before the next one`, `a batch job failed: its selection rounds kept failing`); the pass logs nothing for `Stepped::Backoff`. **What is shown, and by which test** (the first version of this decision said more than its test showed): `the_prefix_of_a_batch_selector_whose_selection_fails_reaches_only_the_job_that_holds_it` hands the executor an error whose text quotes the canary prefix (a store of the test) and shows that **this code reads and logs none of it**: not the executor, not the pass, the real store that records the failures, the audit record, the events, the log with its spans, the metric page, every table (the three that hold the prefix on purpose are searched for the text of the error too). It could not show what the storage layer does with a real database error, because the error never went through it; `the_prefix_of_a_batch_selector_quoted_by_a_database_error_of_its_selection_reaches_no_log` does (D2203): a trigger raises an exception whose message quotes the prefix inside the real round, and the same searches are made. `GET /batch-jobs/{id}` already had `failureReason`; it shows `SELECTION_FAILED` and the body has the same fields as a job that has not failed, no count, no backoff and no cause (`rest::a_job_whose_selection_failed_is_read_with_its_reason_and_nothing_else_of_the_failure`, which also reads the list). No OpenAPI change: `SELECTION_FAILED` was in the enumeration since the contract, and the Studio, the CLI and the SDKs already show it.
- **D2203** (2026-11-11, G2094; the reviews of D2197) **The error of a selection round does not reach the log as text: it reaches it as its SQLSTATE.** Three reviews said that D2202's "reaches no log" was not shown: the canary test failed the round above the store, so `store_error` and its `record()` (`infra-postgres/src/identity.rs`, which logs `%detail` of every `Unavailable` and `Internal`) never ran. The test written for it (`privacy::the_prefix_of_a_batch_selector_quoted_by_a_database_error_of_its_selection_reaches_no_log`: a trigger raises an exception whose message quotes the canary inside the real round, the real store, the real executor, both roles, the log with its spans, the metrics, the audit record, the events and every table) **was red first, for the right reason**: `ERROR sagat_infra_postgres::identity: storage failure detail=error returned from database: the statement over the ids that start with canary-failed-selector-… was cancelled`, ten times. The comment of `store_error` says "server messages carry no row values"; that holds for most messages and not for a cast that fails (it names the value) or a function that raises (it names what it is given). Fixed at the source for the selection round only, not by silencing `record()`: the statements of `select_round`, `selection_failed` and `end_failed` map their errors with `store_error_without_text`, which classifies as `store_error` does (a timeout or a deadlock is `Unavailable`, so what the caller decides is the same) and replaces the text by its SQLSTATE as `sqlstate:XXXXX`, never logging it; `StoreError::sqlstate()` reads it back only when it is exactly five upper-case letters and digits (a unit test refuses anything looser), and the executor's two warnings carry `job`, `failures` and `sqlstate` (`57014` a statement over its timeout, `40P01` a deadlock, `P0001` in the test): the diagnostic an operator had in the text, as a closed code. The first test (the store of the test that hands on an error with a quoting text) stays: it is what shows the executor and the pass read none of an error they are handed. What remains of G2094 is every other statement of every store (G2094 says it).
- **D2204** (2026-11-11; correctness review) **The row lock is what protects a cancel from the record of a failure, and a test holds it.** `selection_failed` reads the job `FOR UPDATE`; without it the plain read is right whenever the cancel committed before it (the first cancel test), and wrong when the cancel commits while the record waits: the write that follows waits for the cancel, then overwrites it, and at ten failures `end_failed` moves a `CANCELLING` job to `FAILED` (the guard trigger allows that transition, so the lock is the only protection). `a_cancel_that_commits_while_a_failure_is_recorded_is_not_overwritten_and_the_failure_records_nothing`: a transaction of the test holds the write of the cancel uncommitted (the statement `request_cancel` makes), the record starts, the test waits on `pg_stat_activity` until a backend waits on a lock while running a statement about `batch_jobs` (no sleep), the cancel commits; the record answers `NotSelecting`, the job is `CANCELLING`, the count has not moved and the slot is the cancel's; again with ten failures on record, where the job must not become `FAILED`. Both roles. The hand mutant that removes `FOR UPDATE` was green against every test before it and is red now. (The test's cancel is the statement of `request_cancel` held open, not the method: the method commits by itself; G2101.)
- **D2205** (2026-11-11; correctness review) **A cancel brings the slot of the job back to the database's now.** `request_cancel` set `next_slot_at = LEAST(next_slot_at, $at)` with `$at` the service's clock; the slot of a job that waits (the backoff of a selecting one, the next item of a running one) is ahead of the database's clock, and a service whose clock runs `s` ahead would leave the cancel waiting for about `s`. It is `LEAST(next_slot_at, clock_timestamp())` now, and the port's `request_cancel` has no `at` (it had no other use). No existing test pinned the old value (read: every assertion on `next_slot_at` in the batch tests of the three suites). `the_cancel_of_a_job_that_waits_is_due_at_once_whatever_the_clock_of_the_service_says` (a service clock a minute ahead; a job that waits an hour, and a running job whose next item is a thousand seconds away: the slot is not after the database's now after the cancel and the scan returns the job) was red first: slot a minute ahead of the database's now.
- **D2206** (2026-11-11; all three reviews) **The comments of the due-scan, of the test that pins its hint and of `BatchRound.failed` say what holds now.** "Only a running job's slot is ever ahead; a job in selection is due from its creation" was a false invariant after D2198: a selecting job has a slot ahead while it waits the backoff of a failed round. The comments of `due_jobs_as_of` say that the statement looks for the slot of a running job only, that a selecting job that waits is neither returned nor a wake-up (the loop finds it at its first poll after its time, G2095) and that the probe walks `batch_jobs_due_idx` past every selecting job that waits ahead of it in the shard, so its cost is the position of the first running job plus the number of jobs waiting after a failed round (bounded by the active-job ceilings of the tenants of the shard), not constant as the comment claimed. `a_slot_ahead_of_a_job_that_is_not_running_is_not_a_wake_up` is said to pin what it pins: neither a selecting job nor a cancelling one whose slot is ahead is returned as due or reported as the soonest slot (`DueJobs::default()`). `BatchRound.failed` says a recorded selection failure counts. The comment of `SagatBatchJobsStalled` lists the causes that are true now.
- **D2207** (2026-11-11; architecture review) **The failure policy is in the spec.** A user-visible policy (ten retries, 5 s doubling to 5 min, the job ends about 25 minutes after its first failure) existed only as D2197. `spec.md` gains, additively: acceptance scenario 6 of US05, a sentence of FR-018-029 (the round is recorded durably, the backoff, ten failed rounds survived, the eleventh ends the job, the text of the error reaches nothing, the default is the plan's and the owner's to confirm), and SC-018-018; no existing requirement is reworded. O1953 asks the owner to accept or change ten; it is not written to `docs/owner-decisions.md`. `docs/traceability.md` keeps no rows for feature 018 (read: it has none for FR-018 or SC-018), so none changes.
- **D2208** (2026-11-11; architecture review, G2097 closed) **A job that fails its selection has an alert of its own.** `SagatBatchJobsStalled` is `batch_jobs_active > 0 unless rate(batch_items_total[10m]) > 0`: other healthy jobs keep the rate above zero, so a failing selection was silent. `SagatBatchSelectionFailed` is `sum(increase(batch_selection_failures_total{outcome="failed"}[15m])) > 0`, severity `warning` (the file's convention for a thing to look at; `critical` is for what pages), no `for` (a job ended: an event, which stays in the window for a quarter of an hour), runbook `batch-operations.md#51-…`. Its rule tests are in `infra/observability/tests/batch_stalled_test.yaml`, the one file the CI step `promtool test rules` runs (the other two test files of that directory are not run by CI: ADR-0083), and cover: the alert for the quarter of an hour after a job ended and its end, the silence for `outcome="retry"`, and the case that is the reason for the alert (other jobs get outcomes, `SagatBatchJobsStalled` stays silent, this fires). Red first: `promtool test rules` with the cases and no rule failed ("got: []"); with the rule it passes, and the hand mutant that evaluates `outcome="retry"` is red. Run with the Prometheus image pinned in `compose.observability.yaml` (`prom/prometheus:v3.15.0`, read-only mounts): `check rules` (34 rules), `check config`, `test rules` of the three files. The contract test of the alerts (`contract_observability_operations`) lists the new alert with its runbook section, which it checks exists. The runbook says that a burst of `failed` across many jobs means the database, not the selectors.
- **D2209** (2026-11-11; correctness and architecture reviews) **The `down` refuses only for a job that is still selecting; the slot column says what it means; mixed versions.** (1) A job that ended `SELECTION_FAILED` keeps its count (11) as evidence, and its `failure_reason` already records it; a guard on every count would have refused every rollback for the retention of every job that ever failed (the purge, T018-051, is unbuilt). The `down` now refuses only when a job with `state = 'SELECTING'` has a count above zero (it would lose its progress to the bound), with a message that says so and what to do (let it end, at most about 25 minutes, or cancel it, or keep a compatible reader); the count of a job that failed goes with the column, its reason stays. No `lock_timeout` was added: no other migration of the repository sets one on its `LOCK TABLE` (the one `lock_timeout` is inside a function for a specific purpose). Red first: the migration test of the rollback failed with the old message when only a job that ended `SELECTION_FAILED` and one that is cancelling held a count. (2) `COMMENT ON COLUMN batch_jobs.next_slot_at` names both meanings (the rate slot of a running job; the end of the wait after a failed selection round), and the `down` takes the comment away (tested; red first: no comment). (3) **Mixed versions**: an older engine that picks a page of a job does not reset the count, so after a rolling deploy a job that is still selecting can carry a count that a pick should have reset, and fail up to ten rounds earlier than it would have; accepted, in the migration, the runbook and here. No change to the storage classification and none to the grants (the privilege audit and `integration_platform_foundation` run in both roles).
- **D2210** (2026-11-11; correctness review) **The scan test asserts the wait it relies on.** `a_job_that_waits_leaves_the_scan_until_its_time_and_the_jobs_behind_it_are_reached` overrode the wait with an hour after the record, so its "catches a failed round that leaves the job due" was not what it showed. It now asserts, before the override, that the slot the record wrote is ahead of the start of the round by the wait of the first failure by the database's clock (`slot >= before + 5 s`, independent of the machine's speed), and says in its doc what it catches. The hand mutant that writes no wait, which survived it, is red now.
- **D2211** (2026-11-11, T018-075, G2066) **The second quickstart pass ran on a stack built from the tree, and what it added to the page.** Commit `83447218`; the compose file of the repository under its own project name (`sagat018qs2`), the three images renamed (`sagat018-qs2/*:dev`) so that no `:dev` tag of another checkout moved, a throw-away environment file outside the repository (the four switches on, and `SAGAT_SECRETS_KEYS` for the webhook step), and one override file with the admin listeners of the control plane and the engine published on the loopback of this machine and the repository's own Prometheus (the pinned image, `prometheus.yml` and `alerts.yaml`; the collector, Jaeger and Grafana were not started, their pinned images being absent from the local daemon and nothing being pulled). No default, migration, ADR, contract or SDK changed. `quickstart.md` was rewritten from the real output; `verification.md` has a second demo log (b.2) and section (l).
- **D2212** (2026-11-11, G2102, FR-018-049) **The two series of `batch_selection_failures_total` exist at zero as soon as an executor does.** The alert `SagatBatchSelectionFailed` is `sum(increase(batch_selection_failures_total{outcome="failed"}[15m])) > 0`, and `increase` does not count the first sample of a series: on the stack the series appeared with the value 1 after the first job ended `SELECTION_FAILED` (samples `1,1,1,1,1,1`, `increase` 0, the alert `inactive`), so the alert would have stayed silent for the first job each engine process ended. `BatchExecutor::new` (no longer a `const fn`) registers `retry` and `failed` with `absolute(0)` (`register_selection_failure_series`), which creates the counters at zero and never lowers a count; the alert expression is **not** changed (the rule tests of D2208 start from zero, which is now what an engine exports). Not done instead: an `offset`/`or vector(0)` rewrite of the rule (harder to read and to test than a counter that exists), and a per-label `describe`. Test first, seen failing: `batch_selection_failures::the_two_series_of_the_selection_counter_exist_at_zero_before_the_first_failure` (`left: None`), green after; on the rebuilt stack both series are `0` on the engine's port before any job and the alert was `firing` after one ended (`increase` 1.02). Other counters have the same property: G2105.
- **D2213** (2026-11-11, G2103) **A job whose selection fails is finished when the failure is recorded, not when the round began.** `record_failed_round` used the reading of the clock the step began with, so a round that ended in the ten-second statement timeout gave `finished_at` (and the time of the audit record `batch.finished`) up to ten seconds before the job was ended: on the stack `finishedAt` was 11:04:56 for a job that was read `SELECTING` at 11:05:02 and logged its end at 11:05:06. It reads the clock itself now (`select` and `record_failed_round` lose the parameter that carried the early reading). Test first, seen failing with the two instants equal: `batch_selection_failures::a_job_that_fails_its_selection_is_finished_when_the_failure_is_recorded_not_when_the_round_began` (a clock that moves a second at each reading, so a time written from an early reading is told from one written from a later one); on the rebuilt stack `finished_at` 11:18:45.932551 and the log line 11:18:45.936.
- **D2214** (2026-11-11, G2093, O1953) **How the failing selection was made on the stack, and what was not waited for.** Nothing of another project was touched: as the owner of this stack's database, a trigger on `batch_items` whose function sleeps 30 s in each insert (`CREATE FUNCTION … PERFORM pg_sleep(30)`, `CREATE TRIGGER … BEFORE INSERT ON batch_items`), so that the round's own `SET LOCAL statement_timeout` (ten seconds) cancels it, which is a real SQLSTATE `57014`, not a raise of the test; the trigger and the function were dropped at the end of each run (`pg_trigger` and `pg_proc` counted zero). The real backoff to the bound is about 25 minutes and was **not waited for**: after four and after one failed rounds on record, the owner set `batch_jobs.selection_failures` to 10 (the count that ten failed rounds in a row would leave), and the next failed round, the eleventh, ended the job. What that shows is the end, its audit record, its log line, its metric and the alert; it does not show ten real rounds in a row (the integration test does). Statement timeouts of the role (`ALTER ROLE … SET statement_timeout`) were not used: the round sets its own `SET LOCAL`, which overrides them.
- **D2215** (2026-11-11, G2066) **The webhook body of step 2.4 is read from the row of the delivery.** The platform never delivers to a loopback or a private address, so no receiver can be allowed on this machine, and nothing was sent to the internet. The subscription (a secret of the namespace, an egress rule, a URL on `receiver.example.com`, which does not resolve) makes the deliveries, which stay `PENDING` with the outcome `NETWORK` and are retried; `webhook_deliveries.payload` holds the bytes a receiver is sent. A *delivered* webhook with its signature was therefore not shown on the stack (the integration test `run_provenance` receives it).
- **D2216** (2026-11-11) **What the pass corrected in the text of the page.** The batch snippet waits a second before it builds `createdBefore` from `date` (the selector the first try built took the twelve older open runs and none of the twelve new ones: they were created in the same whole second); the delayed run is `reminder-10`, not `reminder-9`; the REST name of the last time of a coalesced or missed summary is `summaryLastNominalTime`; `schedule update` takes the document again, so with no prefix flag and none in the file the action has no prefix any more; `sagat schedule trigger` prints the outcome and, for a held id, no reason (G2104); the refusals of the CLI print its usage after the message. None changes the product.
- **D2217** (2026-11-11, G2105, FR-018-044) **The counters of this feature's own alerts are on the page at zero once their pass exists.** `TriggerPass::new` registers `trigger_schedule_reconcile_failures_total` and `cron_legacy_fires_after_handover_total{source="legacy_pass"}` at zero (`register_alert_series`), as `BatchExecutor::new` does for the selection counter (D2212): `SagatTriggerScheduleReconcileFailing` and `SagatCronLegacyFiredAfterHandover` are `increase(…[15m]) > 0`, which does not count the first sample of a series, so the first failed reconciliation and the first fire of an old engine binary after the handover (a tripwire) would have gone untold. `counted::the_counters_the_trigger_alerts_read_exist_at_zero_before_their_first_event` failed first (no such series on the page) and fails against a registration of one of the two only (hand mutant, restored). Not shown on a stack: the promtool rule tests feed their own series. The counters of other features' alerts are not touched (G2105).
- **D2218** (2026-11-13, G2086, FR-018-044, OD-2026-11-13-3) **A business-id conflict is audited as `business_id.conflict`, with the closed reason and never the id, where the refusal is decided.** (1) **A person's start** (`start`, a with-start whose policy is `FAIL`, a rerun that keeps the id, a reset, a batch `RERUN` item, the completion of a waiting reset): the store refuses with `BusinessIdHeld` and the transaction of the start rolls back, as it must (nothing of a refused admission commits, ADR-0050 decision 2). The record cannot be in that transaction, so it is written by `RunService::start_with` in a transaction of its own (`RunStore::record_refusal`, one insert, no lock), after the rollback and before the `409` is returned, the way a worker's refused binding is recorded (`record_unbound`). Not `record_denial`: that write is fire-and-forget, dropped under load and collapsed, which is right for a cross-tenant probe and wrong for a conflict the owner asked to see. A failure of the write is logged and the answer stays `409` (the refusal does not depend on it). The record is by the person, on the workflow they asked to start (`resourceType` `workflow`: there is no run, and naming the holder would tell an audit reader which execution holds an id they may not read), with the reason `BUSINESS_ID_IN_USE` or `BUSINESS_ID_REUSE_REJECTED`. (2) **A schedule's decision**: the action row of a held id and the audit record commit in the decision's transaction (`ScheduleStore::record_decision` now takes a list of records, so a trigger-now writes its `schedule.triggered` and the conflict together), by the schedule (`system:schedule:<id>`), on the schedule; `start_with` does not write the person's record for a scheduled admission, so one conflict is one record. (3) `USE_EXISTING` (`ALREADY_ACTED`, or a person's start answered with the holder) is no conflict and writes none. Not collapsed: every refused request is its own record, and a client that loops on a held id is bounded by the API's rate limits like any other caller. Tests, red first with no record: `integration … business_id_audit::` (4). *Amended 2026-11-13 by D2220 (the platform's conflicts are recorded by the engine with their fenced decision, not after the rollback) and D2221 (the person's record is collapsed and best effort).*
- **D2219** (2026-11-13, G1961, O1951, FR-018-030, ADR-0050 decisions 2, 3 and 7, OD-2026-11-13-2) **The effect of a batch item on its run and the record of the item are one transaction, the run's.** (1) **The hook** is data, not a closure: the executor builds an `ItemSettlement` (tenant, job, ordinal, fence, the instant, the audit record of the end of the job) and hands it to the action in `ItemRequest.settlement`. Each single-run path a batch item drives takes it through a crate-private twin of its public use case (`operate_item`, `signal_item`, `review_item`, `rerun_item` through `Rerun.item` into `finish_start`, the ad-hoc `start_private` included, and `update_in_own_transaction`), and, after the effect and before `commit`, calls `RunTx::settle_batch_item`, whose PostgreSQL adapter runs the statements `record_item` always ran (`batch::record_item_in`, one function for both) in the run's transaction; once it has committed, the use case says which outcome committed (`ItemSettlement::committed`) and the action answers `ItemEffect::Settled`, which the executor counts and records no more. The public use cases pass none and are unchanged. (2) **The lock order** is the run (3R, the use case's own `begin`), the writes of the effect (as the use case always made them), then the fence of the shard (`FOR SHARE`, the leaf lock of D2083), the job's row and the item's row `FOR UPDATE` (8a), and the quota deltas the transaction flushes when it commits (8b): ADR-0050 decision 3 order (b) and the slots of decision 7, read from the writes of a real item in `batch_thousand` (positions 1 to 13 the effect, 14 and 15 the item and the job, 16 to 18 the quota deltas). No slot-3 row is taken after the run and no second run is locked; the transactions of the batch store that lock the job or an item never lock a run, so no cycle can form. (3) **A refusal there** (the lease is not the replica's, the job no longer runs, the item has ended) is a `StateConflict`; the use case drops the transaction with its effect and answers `AppError::BatchConflict`, which the executor reads as `Stepped::Refused`. The record in the run's transaction takes only a job that is still `RUNNING` (`running_only`): a cancel that committed after the last look stops the effect. `record_item`, in a transaction of its own, still takes a `CANCELLING` job, for what did not touch the run. (4) **What still has a transaction of its own**: a refusal of the action (a skip, a failure, a refusal for now) changed nothing on the run, and is recorded as before; a replay of the key whose effect committed before its item's record existed (only an engine from before this change leaves one) is recorded in the replay's transaction by `operate`, `review`, `signal` and the Update, and by the executor for a rerun, whose replay opens no transaction. `ensure_live` stays as a look that spares a wasted effect; it decides nothing any more. (5) **The Update**: an Update that the run's rule rejects commits its receipt, so its item's `FAILED` (`CONFLICT` for a stale revision, `VALIDATION_FAILED` otherwise, the codes the executor gave it) commits with that receipt too. (6) **Found on the way**: the future of a start, with this and D2218's await added, overflowed the 2 MiB stack of a test thread in a debug build (`fault … firing_decisions::a_process_killed_after_any_write_of_a_buffered_time…`, which already boxed its own future); `start_with` now boxes `finish_start`. `batch_thousand` looked for a transaction that records an item and does not write the run, which no longer exists: it now cuts the run's transaction at the writes of the record and asserts that no run is changed whose item does not say so (the tripwire counts its kills in a sequence, which the kill does not roll back). Tests, red first: `fault … batch_item_transaction::` (3: a cancel in the window, `Acted`; a lease lost in the window, the run `CANCELLED`; a death at the record, the run `CANCELLED` and the item `PENDING`) and `integration … batch_actions::the_effect_of_each_use_case_commits_with_the_record_of_its_item` (the `xmin` of the item and of the effect's audit record differ, `321092` against `321093`). Not measured: the rate of items after the change (one transaction fewer per item, the job's row held to the end of the run's transaction). *Corrected 2026-11-13 by D2222 after two independent reviews.*
- **D2220** (2026-11-13, G2086, FR-018-044, review of D2218) **A business-id conflict the platform meets is recorded by the engine, in the fenced transaction of its decision.** D2218 wrote the record after the rollback whenever the start was not a schedule's, which took in a batch `RERUN` item and the completion of a waiting reset: both named the person (the creator, the requester) for a decision the platform made, and both were written again by every replica that retried after its fenced record was refused (a lease that moved). Now `start_with` writes the post-rollback record only for a person's own request (not a schedule's action, not a batch item, not a pending completion); the batch executor builds the record when the item fails `CONFLICT` and `BatchStore::record_item` commits it with the item (`record_item_in`, after the item and before the end of the job), and the reset completer hands it to `fail_reset` (`ResetFailure.conflict`), which commits it with the failed entry. Both are by `system:engine`, on the run (the run the item reruns, the source of the reset), with the closed reason and the job or the entry (`BUSINESS_ID_IN_USE batch <job>`, `BUSINESS_ID_IN_USE reset <reset>`): ids of the platform, never the business id. The schedule's record now carries the nominal time it decides (`BUSINESS_ID_IN_USE at <time>`), so that the collapse of D2221 never merges two times of one schedule. Tests, red first (the person's record was there: `[("alice", "workflow", …, "BUSINESS_ID_IN_USE")]`): `integration … business_id_audit_platform::` (2), each with the lease taken over while the refusal waits for its fence (the binding held, then the takeover held open, both seen in `pg_stat_activity`): the old replica writes nothing, the new owner one record.
- **D2221** (2026-11-13, G2086, FR-018-044, OD-2026-11-13-3, review of D2218) **The record of a person's refused start is collapsed and best effort: the documented deviation from the letter of FR-018-044.** FR-018-044 says business-id conflicts are audited "in the decision's transaction"; a person's refused start commits no transaction, so its record cannot be in it (D2218), and a person can send the refused start as often as they like. The platform rule for an event a caller repeats at will applies: `business_id.conflict` collapses identical repeats (same tenant, actor, resource and reason) within 300 s, as `database.denied` does (`AuditAction::collapses_repeats`, `repeat_window`). A record that cannot be written is counted, `audit_write_failures_total{action}` (a closed label, the action), and logged, and the answer stays `409`. What this gives up: the trail of a person's conflicts is at most one record per workflow and reason every five minutes, and a database failure between the rollback and the record loses the record (visible in the counter). The platform's records (D2220) are in their decision's transaction and are never lost that way; their reasons carry the time, the job or the entry, so the collapse never merges two decisions. Whether this best effort is acceptable is the owner's to confirm (OD-2026-11-13-3 chose the audit action, not its durability). Test, red first (`left: 5`, `right: 1`): `business_id_audit::a_person_who_repeats_a_refused_start_leaves_one_record_within_the_window`. **Accepted by the owner on 2026-11-13 (OD-2026-11-13-5).**
- **D2222** (2026-11-13, G1961, O1951, review of D2219) **What two independent reviews of D2219 corrected.** (1) **A replay is recorded on a cancelling job.** `settle_batch_item` took only a `RUNNING` job, also for a replay of an effect that an older engine committed without its item; a cancel after the new engine's look then left the item to the drain, `CANCELLED`, though the run had been changed. The use cases now say whether they found the effect already committed (`replay`: the receipt of `operate`, `review` and `signal`, `UpdateResolution::replayed`), and a replay is recorded like `record_item` records it (`running_only: !replay`). Red first: `fault … batch_item_transaction::an_effect_an_older_engine_applied_is_recorded_though_the_job_was_cancelled_since` (`Refused("the job no longer runs this item")`). (2) **A committed outcome stands.** The executor read the answer of the call before the outcome that committed with the effect, so a failure after the commit (`operate_item` reads the run back, a rerun its new run) became `DEFERRED` or `Refused`; it now looks at `settlement.settled()` first. Red first: `…::an_outcome_that_committed_stands_when_the_call_fails_after_the_commit` (`Refused("the batch job is over")`). (3) **An item is recorded only with its own run**: `ItemSettlement` carries the run of the item; the adapter refuses a transaction whose run is neither it nor a rerun of it, and the lock of the item is taken on `run_id` too. `…::an_item_is_recorded_only_with_its_own_run`, red against a hand mutant without either check (`Ok(Settled(Succeeded))`). (4) The tests the reviews asked for: the rollback of a refused record for `SIGNAL`, `MARK_MANUAL_REVIEW`, `RERUN` (a workflow run and an ad-hoc one) and `UPDATE`, not only `CANCEL`; the crash test proves the cut backend held the run's row and had written the journal; the one-`xmin` test covers `CANCEL`, `RESUME`, `RETRY_STEP`, the ad-hoc rerun and a rejected Update.
- **D2223** (2026-11-13, ADR-0050 decisions 3 and 7, FR-018-030, review of D2219) **A batch `RERUN` that keeps its business id takes the binding (slot 3, order (a)) and then the job and the item (8a): a recorded deviation from "order (a) MUST NOT lock a job, item or obligation row", with no cycle.** Observed by the tripwire (`fault … batch_item_transaction::a_rerun_that_keeps_its_id_and_an_update_write_in_the_order_of_the_slots`): the item's transaction writes `business_ids` (the claim), then the new run (`payloads`, `run_active_counts`, `runs`, `audit_records`, `command_dedupe`, `run_events`, `event_outbox`, `steps`), then `batch_items` and `batch_jobs`, the `batch.finished` record of a job it ends, and last `tenant_retained_usage` (8b, flushed at the commit; `quota_transaction_deltas` is a row of the transaction's own, keyed by its xid, which nothing else waits for). The locks of the record are the job's row and then the item's, both `FOR UPDATE`, after the fence. An Update item writes `run_events`, `workflow_operation_states`, `event_outbox`, `operation_update_receipts`, `runs` and its audit record, then the item and the job, then the usage row: order (b), as ADR-0050 says. Why the rerun's deviation has no cycle: every transaction that holds a job's or an item's row (`record_item`, `request_cancel`, `drain_cancel`, `fail_job`, `select_round`, `selection_failed`, `complete_job`) locks no business-id row and no run, so none of them can wait for what the rerun holds; the transactions that take a business-id row first (a start, a schedule's action, a reset's completion) never lock a job or an item, except this one, which takes them last; two rerun items of one job take their bindings and then the same job row, in one order. What the rerun keeps of order (b): it locks no second existing run and no slot-3 row after a run. ADR-0050 decision 7 now lists it beside the recorded deviation of `CANCEL_SOURCE`.
- **D2240** (2026-11-14, OD-2026-11-14-4, OD-2026-11-14-5, FR-018-053, ADR-0091 decision 1, T018-082/084) **The automatic handover is a one-shot job with the owner's credentials that runs the tested command.** `control-plane schedule-trigger-handover --auto` is the operator's `--apply` (the same census, the same activation transaction, the same refusals) with the conditions of D2241 to D2244 and a bounded wait; a compose service and a Helm `post-install,post-upgrade` hook Job run it (D2250). The runtime role gets no new privilege and ADR-0082 is unchanged: the job is the operator's own command, run on its behalf with the operator's credential. The first draft (a `SECURITY DEFINER` function the engines call) was rejected after the three reviews of 2026-11-14 because `sagat_app` writes `scheduler_members` (`20261017000000_scheduler_shards.up.sql:92`) and `instance_register` takes the kind, owner and capabilities from its caller (`20270113000000_instance_registry.up.sql:180-183`, `:228-236`), so it would decide on facts its caller can forge; an engine-only role keeps that forgery; `migrate` waiting deadlocks (the engines start after it, `infra/docker/compose.yaml:162-164`, `:205-207`); the control plane at start-up runs as the runtime role; a long-running owner process keeps the credential for the platform's life; deferring to 0.4.0 was not the owner's choice.
- **D2241** (2026-11-14, FR-018-053, ADR-0091 decision 2) **The job counts engines over a horizon and waits for stable rounds; `--expect-live` stays for the operator.** A round is the census read as the owner; `--auto` hands over after `--stable N` consecutive rounds (default 3, at least 15 s apart, more than one heartbeat) in which the set of live engine instances is the same, has at least `--min-live M` members (default 1; Helm passes the engine's `replicas` or its autoscaler's minimum), and every engine row seen within `--horizon` (default 10 min, at least 60 s; a row silent within it counts) lists `schedules-v1`; the activation then runs with `expect_live` set to the stable count, so a change after the last round refuses it (`handover.rs:184-189`, `:297-325`). Trade-off: `--expect-live {{ replicas }}` is exact but false under an autoscaler (`infra/helm/values.yaml:165-180`) and during a rollout; the horizon and the stable rounds hold under autoscaling but cannot see an engine that stopped cleanly (its row and membership are deleted: G2245), so a fleet that is partly down above `M` hands over. Given, `--expect-live` replaces `--min-live`. *Revised after the second review (D2256):* no handover before one horizon after the first round; the horizon is at most an hour.
- **D2242** (2026-11-14, G2005, ADR-0091 decision 2) **An unregistered scheduler membership blocks the job (`ENGINE_UNREGISTERED`).** A 0.1 or 0.2 engine does not register but its keeper announces it in `scheduler_members` (`v0.2.0:crates/infra-postgres/src/partitioning.rs`, lines 51-60; `v0.2.0:apps/execution-engine/src/lib.rs`, line 168); an engine of this release registers with `scheduler_owner` equal to its member name (`apps/execution-engine/src/lib.rs:195-200`). The job reads the memberships as the owner and counts as present every row whose `expires_at` is within the horizon, expired or not, whose owner no engine row seen within the horizon names. It replaces, for `--auto`, the attestation of step 1 of runbook 6.2. The runtime role can write both tables (G2243).
- **D2243** (2026-11-14, G2020, ADR-0091 decision 2) **Recent work of the reconciliation blocks the job (`RECONCILIATION_RECENT`).** Publication and reconciliation make a trigger schedule through the same `insert` (`crates/infra-postgres/src/trigger_schedules.rs:150-210`), so nothing tells them apart today. The migration adds a nullable `schedules.reconciled_at`, which the reconciliation sets when it makes, revives or removes a trigger-owned schedule (never a publication), with a partial index; the job waits until no mark is younger than the horizon. Only a control plane older than this release leaves that work to the reconciliation; the triggers older than the feature leave the same mark after an upgrade, so the job hands over a horizon after their backlog is made. It narrows G2020 (G2241) and replaces the quiet window of the first draft. *Revised after the second review (D2258):* the mark is also set on a respecification and a resume.
- **D2244** (2026-11-14, OD-2026-11-14-3, ADR-0091 decision 2) **`--auto` waits for a live control plane (`NO_CONTROL_PLANE`).** The control planes come last in the upgrade order; one of this release registered says that their step has begun. The operator's `--apply` does not require it (the operator attests it, runbook 6.2 step 1).
- **D2245** (2026-11-14, FR-018-053, ADR-0091 decision 5) **The reasons are one closed set, kept in a row and exported by the engines; the alert does not restart.** `ENGINE_LACKS_CAPABILITY`, `ENGINE_UNREGISTERED`, `TOO_FEW_ENGINES`, `FLEET_CHANGING`, `RECONCILIATION_RECENT`, `NO_CONTROL_PLANE`, `UNCONVERGED`, `BUSY`, `DEADLINE`. A one-shot job cannot be scraped, so each round writes the last reason, its count and the time to the owner-written singleton `schedule_trigger_handover_attempt`, which `sagat_app` reads; every engine exports `trigger_schedule_handover_blocked{reason}` from it while `LEGACY` (series created at zero, D2217). `SagatTriggerHandoverBlocked` is `max without (reason) (...) == 1` for one hour, so a change of reason keeps its timer (correctness review). A test holds the SQL codes, the Rust enum, the runbook anchors and the rows of runbook 6.3 to one set. With the opt-out no job writes the row and nothing fires. Ids go to the log, never to a label. *Revised after the second review (D2254, D2255):* the reasons add `FIRST_HORIZON`, `CHECK_TOO_SLOW` and `INTERRUPTED`; the mode metric is exported by the engines.
- **D2246** (2026-11-14, OD-2026-11-14-7, ADR-0091 decision 7, constitution VI) **The handover is audited by mode and epoch, with the census copied and one tenant row per affected tenant after the commit.** The handover row gains `changed_mode` (`OPERATOR` | `AUTOMATIC`; the migration backfills `OPERATOR` on a row already handed over, so a mode is null only in `LEGACY`) and `changed_epoch`. In the activation's transaction the census (instance id, kind, binary version, `started_at`, `last_seen` of every process seen within the horizon) is copied into an append-only, owner-only child table keyed by the epoch: the history row is generic and append-only, and its insert is the registry's function. After the commit the command writes one `audit_records` row per tenant that has a cron trigger, the id derived from the tenant and the epoch, `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING`; a run that finds the state handed over writes the missing rows of the recorded epoch first, so a job that died between the commit and the rows is completed by its retry or the next run. One log line gives the mode, the epoch, the engines and the count of tenants. It replaces the "no tenant audit entry" of D2042; nothing is written under the table lock. *Revised after the second review (D2252, D2253, D2255, D2257):* the census is copied whole or refused; the tenant set is recorded in the transaction with random audit ids and completed only from it, with the handover row's actor, mode and time; the counter is replaced by the engines' gauge.
- **D2247** (2026-11-14, OD-2026-11-14-6, O1954, ADR-0091 decision 6) **The opt-out is a deployment value.** `schedules.autoHandover: false` leaves the Job out of the Helm chart; `SAGAT_SCHEDULES_AUTO_HANDOVER=false` makes the compose service exit 0 at once saying the handover is left to the operator. On by default. No engine reads either, so replicas cannot disagree.
- **D2248** (2026-11-14, OD-2026-11-14-4, ADR-0091 Consequences) **What the release notes of 0.3.0 say about the handover.** That it is automatic after a deployment, its conditions, and how to see it happened (the gauge, the log, the row with its mode, the tenant audit); one-way (no command back, the schema `down` refuses, no engine below 0.3.0 after it); the opt-out of D2247; `helm upgrade --timeout` longer than the job's wait (G2246); the accepted losses G2005, G2020 and G2021 in a sentence each; that an engine with `SAGAT_SCHEDULES_ENABLED=false` holds it.
- **D2249** (2026-11-14, ADR-0091 decisions 3 and 4) **No transaction is open across a wait, and timeouts are a retried `BUSY`.** A round is short reads; the wait between rounds holds no connection; the activation is the existing bounded transaction, attempted only after a stable round. `55P03` (the epoch's `FOR UPDATE` in `platform_requirement_activate`), `57014`, `HANDOVER_BUSY` and the activation's step bound (`within_the_step`, `crates/infra-postgres/src/instances.rs`) map to `BUSY`, retried at the next round (today the first two are database errors with exit code 1: `handover.rs:481-494`, `apps/control-plane/src/lib.rs:96-110`). The statement timeout (3 s) exceeds the longest single lock wait (2 s) plus its work; the step bound (3 s) exceeds the waits while the epoch is held (the table, 2 s; the handover row does not wait, two handovers serialise on the epoch) plus the work; both stay under the 5 s statement timeout of the processes that wait for the epoch (ADR-0082 decision 2). *Revised after the second review (D2254):* refusals are typed, retried with a backoff, and three statement timeouts are `CHECK_TOO_SLOW`; the rounds run with 5 s.
- **D2250** (2026-11-14, OD-2026-11-14-5, ADR-0091 decision 1) **The shape of the two jobs and their exit codes.** Exit 0: handed over, already handed over, switched off; 2: declined for a reason no wait cures (the runtime role's credential, a state this build does not know); 3: the deadline, with the last reason; 1: a database failure. Compose: a service `schedule-trigger-handover` on the control-plane image with the owner's `DATABASE_URL`, `restart: "no"`, after `control-plane` and `execution-engine` have started (they have no health check, G2247), `--auto --wait 2h`. Helm: a Job with `helm.sh/hook: post-install,post-upgrade`, `hook-delete-policy: before-hook-creation`, `backoffLimit: 2`, `activeDeadlineSeconds` the wait plus five minutes, a `podFailurePolicy` that fails it at once on exit codes 2 and 3, the owner Secret `schedules.autoHandover.ownerDatabaseSecret`, `--min-live` from the engine's replicas or autoscaler minimum, and `--operator` naming the release. *Revised after the second review (D2251, D2252, D2254):* Helm runs a plain Job of the release, not a hook, as `sagat_handover`, with its own service account; exit code 3 is the deadline, 4 an interruption; the `podFailurePolicy` fails on 2 only.
- **D2251** (2026-11-14, second review Major 1, ADR-0091 decision 1) **The Helm job is a plain Job of the release, named by its revision, and stops on SIGTERM before an activation.** A `post-upgrade` hook makes `helm upgrade` wait (default `--timeout` 5 min) for a job that waits at least a horizon (10 min) on an upgrade from 0.2, so the upgrade fails; with `--atomic` the images return to 0.2, and a hook is not part of the release, so the Job goes on looping for its wait. A plain Job (`<release>-schedule-trigger-handover-<revision>`, `ttlSecondsAfterFinished` 1 h) is not waited for (without `--wait-for-jobs`), and the next upgrade or a rollback deletes the Job of the revision it leaves. After SIGTERM or SIGINT the command starts no activation: it ends its round, writes `INTERRUPTED`, exits 4; an activation already begun is bounded at 3 s and commits or rolls back whole inside the 30 s grace period. Departs from the letter of OD-2026-11-14-5 ("a Helm `post-upgrade` hook"): O1957. Supersedes the hook of D2250 and withdraws O1956. *Revised after the third review (D2268):* the name is cut and hashed to 63 characters, `restartPolicy: Never`, a drain is ignored by the failure policy, and the pod goes with the Job.
- **D2252** (2026-11-14, second review Major 2, ADR-0091 decision 1, ADR-0082 decision 1) **The job runs as `sagat_handover`, a login role that can make this handover and nothing else.** The chart held no owner credential (`infra/helm/templates/NOTES.txt:11`); the owner's would be in the cluster at every upgrade and can do anything. `sagat_handover` (`NOSUPERUSER NOCREATEDB NOCREATEROLE NOREPLICATION`, owns nothing) is created as `sagat_app` is, by `infra/docker/postgres-init` in compose and once by the operator elsewhere (a migration cannot carry a password, and the migrating role may lack `CREATEROLE`: G2249); the new migration grants it, inside `IF EXISTS`, `EXECUTE` on the round functions, a wrapper that records `schedules-v1` for `EXECUTION_ENGINE` and moves the state (calling the owner-only functions as their owner) and the audit completion function, and `SELECT` on the handover and attempt rows; no table write, not `platform_requirement_activate` or `_remove`. This amends ADR-0082 decision 1 ("the operator command runs with the schema-owning role") for this command only; "never the runtime role's" stands. The chart `required`s `schedules.autoHandover.databaseSecret` when the job is on and renders nothing that names it when off, so a pod never hangs on a missing Secret; the Job has its own service account with `automountServiceAccountToken: false`; the runbook says to delete the Secret once handed over. Departs from the letter of OD-2026-11-14-5 ("with the owner's credentials"): O1957. *Revised after the third review (D2259, D2264, D2265, D2266):* every `migrate` run ensures the role and its grants; the role can make only the automatic handover, which the database checks; it is retired with `NOLOGIN`; what it can run is audited.
- **D2253** (2026-11-14, second review Majors 3 and 4, OD-2026-11-14-7) **The tenant set is recorded in the activation's transaction, each with a random audit id, and the rows are completed only from it.** `sagat_app` may insert into `audit_records` (`20261008000200_runtime_role_grants.up.sql:20`), whose key is `id` (`20261008000000_identity.up.sql:56`), so an id derived from the tenant and a guessable epoch could be planted first and `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` would keep the forgery. The activation writes, owner-only and keyed by the epoch, one row per tenant with a cron trigger and a `gen_random_uuid()` audit id the runtime role cannot read; after the commit the completion function writes each row not yet audited with that id and the actor, mode and time of the handover row, and marks it; an id found present and different is refused `AUDIT_CONFLICT`. A tenant that gained its first cron trigger after the commit is not audited; one that lost its last still is. Revises D2246 and closes most of G2244; the set's cost under the lock is G2248.
- **D2254** (2026-11-14, second review Minors 8 and 9, ADR-0091 decision 4) **Refusals are typed, and what a wait cures is retried with a backoff.** The command returns a reason, never a matched text; `Declined` (exit 2) is kept for what no wait cures. A change of the live count under the lock (`handover.rs:305`), a state conflict (`:478`), `HANDOVER_BUSY` (`:483`), `55P03`, `57014` and the step bound are retried by `--auto` with a backoff of 15 s doubling to 5 min; three `57014` in a row are `CHECK_TOO_SLOW`. The rounds run with a statement timeout of 5 s, since the convergence count gives itself 4 s (`crates/infra-postgres/src/trigger_schedules.rs:786`); the activation keeps 3 s. One test per `declined(` call site (16 today) holds its exit code. Revises D2249 and D2250.
- **D2255** (2026-11-14, second review Minor 10, ADR-0091 decision 5) **The engines export the mode of the handover; the job exports nothing.** `trigger_schedule_handover_mode{mode}` (1 for the `changed_mode` an engine reads, 0 for the other, both created at zero) replaces `trigger_schedule_handovers_total{mode}` of D2246: a one-shot job is not scraped.
- **D2256** (2026-11-14, second review Minor 7 and nits, ADR-0091 decision 2) **No handover before one horizon after the job's first round; the horizon is at most an hour; a retried pod resumes.** Without a backlog the detectors would have nothing to see yet, so `--auto` waits `FIRST_HORIZON`. The horizon is between 60 s and 1 h, because expired memberships are deleted after an hour (`crates/infra-postgres/src/partitioning.rs:75`). The first round's time and the deadline are kept in the attempt row under the job's run id (the Job's name); a retried pod with the same id resumes them, another id starts afresh. *Revised after the third review (D2259, D2263):* the horizon's floor is 10 min and is the database's; the run id is the Job's UID, not its name.
- **D2257** (2026-11-14, second review nit, D2246) **The census copy never truncates.** The registry holds at most 1,000 rows (D2016), so every row seen within the horizon fits; a census that does not fit is a defect and refuses the handover.
- **D2258** (2026-11-14, second review Minor 6, D2243) **The reconciliation marks every change it makes to a trigger-owned schedule.** `schedules.reconciled_at` is set on `Make`, `Revive`, `Respec` (`crates/infra-postgres/src/trigger_schedules.rs:877-946`), `Resume` (`:1020`) and the removal of a gone trigger's schedule, each with a test; never by a publication.
- **D2259** (2026-11-14, third review Major 1, ADR-0091 decisions 1, 2 and 9) **The database decides the automatic handover; the job only asks.** Under the lock the capability and live-count checks were the Rust `judge` (`apps/control-plane/src/handover.rs:297-325`) and the SQL checked only the requirement and convergence (`20270114000000_trigger_handover.up.sql:175-185`), so a holder of the `sagat_handover` password could call the wrapper from `psql`, skip the detectors, `--min-live` and the first horizon, and name the actor and the mode.
