The `architecture-reviewer` then read the final diff (with the fixes above): ADR-0091 and ADR-0082 amended in text (M1), `trigger_auto_handover::the_runtime_role_may_only_read_the_handover_row_the_attempt_rows_and_the_audit_count` added (M2), compose's floor and the release notes (m5, m6); G2313 to G2315 keep the rest.
- **D2312** (2026-11-15, T018-087, G2308, OD-2026-11-15-12) **A tenant's audit record of the handover names `operator`, not the operator.** `schedule_trigger_handover_audit()` writes (and compares a record found under a drawn id against) the actor `operator` when the mode is `OPERATOR`, and the handover row's actor otherwise (the job's `system:handover-job:<run id>`, which names a run, not a person). The name typed after `--operator` stays in the handover row and in `platform_requirement_history`, which no tenant reads. *Test, red first (no record with the actor `operator`):* `trigger_auto_handover::the_operators_handover_records_mode_operator_and_its_audit_too` (one record of `operator`, none of the name in `audit_records`, the name in the history, a second completion writing nothing).
- **D2313** (2026-11-15, T018-088, G2250, G2289, OD-2026-11-15-14) **The handover Job is run in a kind cluster for a release.** `tests/e2e/scalability-ha/kind-handover.sh` makes a kind cluster of its own (kind v0.33.0, `kindest/node:v1.37.0` pinned by digest), loads the control-plane and engine images built from the tree, runs PostgreSQL 16 (pinned by digest, the runtime role from `infra/docker/postgres-init`) and `control-plane migrate` with the role's password in the cluster, and installs the chart with the job on (`environment: dev`, one control plane and one engine, `eventTransport: none`, no NetworkPolicy). Then: the Job of revision 1 writes `FIRST_HORIZON` under its UID; `helm upgrade --atomic` to a revision whose engine image does not exist fails and is rolled back, and the script requires that the Job of revision 2 is gone, that the pod of revision 1's Job wrote `INTERRUPTED` (Helm deleted it at the upgrade, so it received SIGTERM), and that the rollback created the Job of revision 1 again under a new UID whose run has a later first round; finally the first round of that run is moved back by a horizon as the owner and the Job completes with `HANDED_OVER AUTOMATIC system:handover-job:<that UID>`. The CI job `kind-handover` runs it only when the run is a release (`needs.changes.outputs.full`), installing kind through the Go module proxy at the pinned version.
- **D2314** (2026-11-18, T018-010, G2065, FR-018-046, the maintainer's decision) **"Assert `workflow.schema.json` and `worker.baseline.json` unchanged" is asserted as the absence of this feature's own vocabulary from both, not as a digest.** A digest of the two files fails for every later feature that changes them on purpose, and the tree has moved since 018 branched (collection operators changed the IR), so "byte-equal to the merge-base" cannot be tested from a checkout; what can be tested, and what the sentence means, is that nothing of this feature is in them but the one declared field. `tests/contract/schedules-bulk-operations/unchanged.rs` (3 tests, target `contract_schedules_bulk_operations`): the workflow IR holds exactly one definition or property whose name has a word of the feature (`schedule`, `business`, `reset`, `nominal`, `backfill`, `batchjob`, `batchitem`, `batchselector`, `startdelay`, `obligation`, compared lower case and without `_`): `/$defs/update/properties/reapplyOnReset`, a boolean that defaults to false and is not required; `worker.baseline.json` and the compiled `worker.proto` hold no message, field, enum, enum value, service or method with one; and a third test plants a name in a copy of each of the three documents and sees it found, so a check that looks in the wrong place is red. Seen failing against three hand mutants of the real files, each restored (`git status` clean): a `scheduleId` property under `$defs/update` of the IR, a `schedule_id` field 77 of `HeartbeatRequest` in `worker.baseline.json`, a `business_id` field in `worker.proto`. **Limits, said:** a later feature that names something with one of the ten words on purpose fails it and edits the list in its own pull request, with the decision that says why; it does not prove that the rest of the two files equals what it was (`crates/api-grpc/tests/compatibility.rs` and the IR contract suites own that). **Closes G2065.**
- **D2315** (2026-11-18, T018-011, specs/AGENTS.md: a task text that names what was never created is amended by a decision) **The ports `BusinessIdStore` and `ResetStore` of T018-011 were never separate traits, and `crates/application/src/runs/business_id.rs` was never created; the work they name is in the ports that carry it.** The claim of a business identifier is an admission of the creation (`NewRun.business: BusinessStart`, with `NewRun.reset_sequence`, `NewRun.pending_reset`, `NewRun.schedule`; the start delay is no field of `NewRun`: it is validated in `Runs` (`START_DELAY_INVALID`) and is the `run.start_delayed` event of the run's first transaction, `execution::start_run_delayed`) processed by `RunStore::create` in the lock order of ADR-0050 decision 7 (`fault … slot_order::`, one test per admission path); the lookup of a binding is `RunStore::business_binding`; the reset register is `RunStore::{reset_source, run_resets, run_reset, reset_receipt, reset_made}`, the use cases are `runs/{reset,reapply,pending_reset}.rs`, and the completion of a reset that waited has its own ports `ResetCompletions` and `PendingResetStore` (`reset_completion.rs`). The infrastructure side is `crates/infra-postgres/src/business_ids.rs` (T018-034, ticked). Two more traits that only forwarded to these would be the empty mappers rule 6 forbids. `ScheduleStore`, `ScheduleCalendar` and `BatchStore` are traits, as the task says.
- **D2324** (2026-11-19, T018-051 purge half, G2317, FR-018-033, FR-018-045) **The retention window of a job is the job's own `expires_at`, stamped when the job is created, and it has no setting.** How the other two purges are configured today: the actions of a schedule are kept for the last 1,000 or 30 days, both fixed in the SQL of `schedule_purge_actions`, no setting; a business-id binding carries its own window (`reuse_window_s`, 30 days by default, at most 366), stamped at the start, and the namespace setting of FR-018-020 is read nowhere (G2085). Neither is an environment variable or a namespace setting, so the batch purge follows the second: `BatchService` stamps `expires_at = created_at + retention` (default `DEFAULT_RETENTION`, 30 days, held between `MIN_RETENTION` and `MAX_RETENTION`, 90 days, by `with_retention`, which no code in `crates/`, `apps/` or `tests/` calls, so every job is stamped 30 days), and the purge reads the stamp. FR-018-045's "min(request, namespace, deployment)": no route takes a retention and no namespace setting exists, and adding either is a contract change this slice does not make (no new route, SDK surface or OpenAPI change), so the effective value is the deployment's, held in the range by the service; a request or namespace value added later narrows the stamped value before it is written and the purge does not change. An environment variable was rejected: it would be a third place that must agree with the stamps of jobs already written, and a purge that reads a setting would delete jobs earlier than the `expiresAt` their creators were shown. Tests: `batch::a_job_is_created_selecting_with_its_limits_and_an_audit_record` (30 days) and `batch_purge::a_job_inside_its_window_is_kept_with_every_item_and_one_past_it_by_an_hour_goes`.
- **D2325** (2026-11-19, T018-051 purge half, G2317, FR-018-033) **A job is purged when it has finished, `expires_at` has passed and it ended at least 14 days ago; its items go with it; a job that has not finished is never purged.** `data-model.md` said "`expires_at` after terminal" and the brief of this slice said "`finished_at` older than the window"; the two readings differ for a job that ends late. Counting the window from the end alone (`finished_at + (expires_at - created_at)`) makes the `expiresAt` the read of the job shows wrong for every job by its running time; reading `expires_at` alone removes a job that ended after its own window (it waited for an engine that was off, or the selection waited for a transaction, G1963: the wait has no deadline) in the first housekeeping pass after it ended, before anyone could read what it did. So both hold: the instant the creator was shown, and a floor of 14 days after the end (the number of D2326). Every comparison is the database's `now()`, never a service clock (the lesson of D2205): the function takes no instant. The three terminal states are named and the state is also the predicate of the partial index; a job that has not finished has no `finished_at` (`batch_jobs_finished_consistent`), so it is also kept out by the test of its age, and the guard of the table (`a batch job that has not finished is never purged`) refuses the delete besides: three layers, tested by the first two (`batch_purge::a_job_that_has_not_finished_is_never_purged_whatever_its_age`, the guard by the owner's refused `DELETE`) and by one hand mutant that removes the state and the age together (M2 in `verification.md` (q)); removing the state alone changes nothing a test can see (M2b, an equivalent mutant: it only costs the index). The `expiresAt` boundary and the floor are each tested an hour either side.
- **D2326** (2026-11-19, T018-051 purge half, G2317; **the reason for the Rust clamp is corrected by D2331**: read that first) **A job is stamped a window of at least 14 days (`MIN_RETENTION`, was one day), twice the 7 days the key of the request that made it is remembered; the purge holds the same floor in SQL.** `BatchService::with_retention` clamped to one day and nothing calls it; the idempotency records are purged after 7 days (`SchedulerSettings::idempotency_retention`, no environment variable), so a window under 7 days would leave the key of a request alive after its job went, and `Applied::Replayed` would then meet no job (`AppError::Internal`, as every replay of a resource that went does: `definitions.rs`, `identity.rs`). With the floor the replay finds the job while the key is remembered and is a request of its own once the key is forgotten (D2329). The number is in two places, the constant and the SQL (`interval '14 days'`), and a test holds them together: `batch_purge::a_job_that_ended_after_its_window_is_kept_for_the_least_retention_after_it_ended` ages a job by `MIN_RETENTION` less and more than an hour (hand mutant R7: 13 days in the constant) and `a_replay_of_the_key_of_a_job_...` asserts `MIN_RETENTION` is longer than the key's memory. The text of `with_retention` ("held inside the hard ceiling") is amended, not reinterpreted: it holds a floor too.
- **D2327** (2026-11-19, T018-051 purge half, G2317, FR-018-033) **The purge is one `SECURITY DEFINER` function, `batch_jobs_purge(p_jobs, p_rows) RETURNS TABLE (jobs_removed, items_removed)`, one job at a time, oldest window first, items in chunks and the job row last.** The shape of `business_ids_purge` and `schedule_purge_actions` (definer, pinned `search_path`, `REVOKE ... FROM PUBLIC`, `GRANT EXECUTE` to `sagat_app` only, no `DELETE` granted), taking one job at a time with `FOR UPDATE OF j SKIP LOCKED` so that a job another replica is purging, or a transaction holds, is passed over and the lock is held only to the end of a call bounded by `p_rows`. The items of the job go by `(tenant_id, job_id, ordinal)` in a chunk of at most the rows the call has left; the row of the job goes only when the chunk did not use the whole budget (items are then all gone) and a row remains for it, so **a call never removes more than `p_rows` rows**, a job is never deleted before its items (the foreign key says so) and a job that the budget cut short keeps its end and the items it has left and stays the oldest, so the next call takes the same job (it resumes; no cursor is kept). A call is one transaction, so a crash leaves the tables as the call before it left them (`fault ... batch_purge::`: the connection is killed after each of the 7 writing statements, with a job taken whole and with a job the call before cut short; the tables are equal, no item is without its job, and the call made again finishes). **Fairness is not a visit list here:** the schedule purge needs `actions_purged_at` because its candidates are every schedule, purgeable or not; the candidates here are only the jobs that are purgeable, so a tenant with nothing to purge is never looked at and cannot hold anyone back; the queue is the order of the windows and every call removes rows, so the backlog drains and no tenant is starved by another except by its place in that queue. The jobs and the items are found by tenant and job, never by the id of a job alone (the id is its tenant's: `batch_purge::only_the_old_finished_jobs_of_every_tenant_and_namespace_go_and_never_the_items_of_the_same_id_elsewhere` seeds the same id in two tenants). The bounds of a call (`BATCH_PURGE_JOBS` 100, `BATCH_PURGE_ROWS` 5,000 rows) are the schedule actions' and the bindings'; how many calls a pass makes (the 20 fixed rounds of the first version) is D2332's; `batch_jobs_purge_idx (expires_at, tenant_id, id) WHERE state IN (terminal)` is what it reads. Hand mutants M1 to M12 are listed in `verification.md` (q).
- **D2328** (2026-11-19, T018-051 purge half, G2317, FR-018-044) **No audit record per purged row; a counter by kind and a counter of failures, both at zero from the start; a failure is logged by its SQLSTATE and nothing else.** What the existing purges do: the actions of a schedule, the bindings of business identifiers, the idempotency records, the worker sessions, the script records and the delivered outbox messages write no audit record and log how many went (`purged ... removed = n`); the housekeeping counts nothing. A purge that wrote one audit record per item would write more than it removes (a job of 100,000 items), the audit trail already holds `batch.created`, `batch.cancelled` and `batch.finished` for the job and each item's effect is audited as the single action is, and what the purge removes is a retention of evidence that FR-018-033 says goes; the operator's record of it is the counter and the line (`purged batch jobs and their items past their retention`, `jobs`, `items`). The counters are new: `batch_purged_total{kind}` (`job`, `item`, two closed words) and `batch_purge_failures_total`, registered at zero when the scheduler is built, because Prometheus's `increase` does not count the first sample of a series (G2102, D2212, D2217); `batch_purge::the_series_of_the_purge_exist_at_zero_before_the_first_purge`. The adapter reads the error with `store_error_without_text` (the statement runs over the selector and the reason, customer text; the text of a database error can quote them, G2094, D2203) and the pass logs `error.sqlstate()` only: `batch_purge::a_failure_of_the_purge_is_counted_and_logged_by_its_sqlstate_and_the_housekeeping_goes_on` makes a trigger raise with the reason of the job in its message and finds it in no log line and no metric, the failure counted once and the rest of the housekeeping done. The privacy test (`...::the_prefix_and_the_reason_of_a_purged_job_reach_no_log_span_metric_or_audit_record_and_are_gone_afterwards`) makes a job by the service with a canary prefix and reason, ages it, purges it through the housekeeping inside a capture of logs, spans and metrics, and searches every table of the schema before (only `batch_jobs`) and after (none).
- **D2329** (2026-11-19, T018-051 purge half, G2317, G2322) **A replay of the key of a purged job cannot meet a missing job, and is a request of its own once the key is forgotten.** `BatchService::create` answers a replay with the job the key made (`store.job`, then `AppError::Internal` if there is none, the pattern of every `Applied::Replayed`); the records of keys are purged after 7 days and a job is kept for 14 at least (D2326), so the key goes first. `batch_purge::a_replay_of_the_key_of_a_job_finds_the_job_while_it_is_kept_and_is_a_new_request_once_both_are_gone`: a job made by the service, four days old and ended three days ago with a window of one day (past its window, inside the floor) is purged by no housekeeping and its key still answers it; at 20 days (ended 15 days ago) the housekeeping removes the key and the job, and the same request under the same key makes a new job, not the one that went. The limit, said: the guarantee is the two numbers; an idempotency retention made configurable above 14 days would break it (G2322).
- **D2330** (2026-11-19, review round, security 3, correctness 3; T018-051 purge half) **One call is measured, its delete was quadratic and is now a range, the rows per call stay 5,000, and the function holds its own ceilings.** The first measurement (`batch_purge::one_call_of_the_purge_measured`, `--ignored --nocapture`, three runs of each case, load average 4 to 14) found that the chunk of items was deleted by a join of the rows to a list of the ordinals the call had chosen, which the planner ran as a nested loop: **5,000 item rows of one job took 3.2 s**, most of the engine's 5-second `statement_timeout` (`PoolConfig`), and 20,000 rows took 49.6 s (`a_call_over_one_large_job_takes_time_in_proportion_to_its_rows` is red for it). A statement that times out rolls the whole call back and the next pass repeats it identically, for ever (G2323). The chunk is now a range of the primary key of the job's own items (`ordinal <= the v_left-th ordinal`, found by an index-only `OFFSET`): **5,000 item rows of one job take 4 to 6 ms; 100 jobs with the heaviest rows (a selector of about 65,000 bytes and a reason of 2,000 characters, which the quota trigger measures at each delete) with their 4,900 items, 43 to 47 ms; 100,000 rows of one job, 89 to 97 ms** (macOS arm64, Docker PostgreSQL 16, one call as the owner; dead tuples are not vacuumed inside the call, so the cost of the vacuum that follows is not in these numbers). A call is far inside one second, so the rows per call stay 5,000 and the jobs 100 (the 1,000 rows suggested in the review were for a call that could exceed one second, and this one does not). **The function is the boundary, so it clamps its own arguments: 1,000 jobs and 100,000 rows a call** (`p_jobs := LEAST(p_jobs, 1000)`, `p_rows := LEAST(p_rows, 100000)`), because the adapter's `i32::MAX` is only the largest the type holds and the runtime role can call the function with any number (`a_call_that_asks_for_more_than_the_function_allows_gets_its_ceilings_and_no_more`, `…_removes_at_most_a_hundred_thousand_rows`: the second job of 100,000 items would be removed with its row if the ceiling were missing). `STRICT`: a call with a `NULL` bound returns no row, which the adapter cannot send; kept so that a caller cannot lift a bound by leaving it out (a note, no behaviour: `a_bound_that_is_not_at_least_one_…`). The partial index does not hold `finished_at`: a job that ended late (past its window but inside the 14-day floor) sits at the head of the index order and is read again at each iteration of a call; they are few (only a job that ended after its window less 14 days), and the read is bounded by the `LIMIT 1` over a sorted index; no index on the expression is possible (`timestamptz + interval` is not immutable).
- **D2331** (2026-11-19, review round, architecture 2 and 6, correctness 6) **The SQL floor alone guarantees that a replay of a key never meets a purged job; the Rust clamp of the stamp is not load-bearing for it; and the 14-day floor is a retention rule.** D2326 gave the clamp as the reason for the guarantee. It is not: the purge makes a job eligible only when `finished_at < now() - 14 days`, a job ends after it was created, so every job outlives its key (7 days) whatever window it carries, and the SQL floor is what holds the guarantee (`a_replay_of_the_key_…`, M7). The clamp (`with_retention` raised from one day to `MIN_RETENTION`) only keeps the `expiresAt` a person is shown honest, so that no job is stamped with a window shorter than the least the purge keeps it (`the_window_a_job_is_stamped_…`, R7b). The two places of the number are held by tests that run on a pull request, not only on a release: a unit test of `infra-postgres` reads the text of the migration (`include_str!`) and asserts it holds `interval '<MIN_RETENTION days> days'`, and a unit test of `scheduler` asserts `SchedulerSettings::default().idempotency_retention < MIN_RETENTION` (hand mutants: the constant at 13 and at 7 days, the literal at 13, the memory of a key at 15 days). **The floor is product-visible:** it keeps a job that ended late past its `expiresAt` and past the 90 days of FR-018-033 (a 30-day job that ends on day 28 lives to day 42; a 90-day job that ends on day 80, to day 94), which the first wording of D2325 and the runbook did not say. It is registered as an owner question with the default built (**O1958**) and one sentence of FR-018-033 marked pending it; `docs/owner-decisions.md` is not written.
- **D2332** (2026-11-19, review round, correctness 1; T018-051 purge half) **The pass calls the purge again while a call used the whole of a bound it was given, until a call finds nothing more, 20 seconds or 100 calls pass.** The first pass was 20 fixed rounds: a job of 100,000 items used the whole budget on its items and its row went at the next pass, 300 seconds later, and in between a read of the job answered `200` with counters that did not match its items. `batch_purge_goes_on(gone, calls, elapsed)` in `passes.rs` is a pure function with unit tests: it goes on when the call removed its jobs bound (100) or its rows bound (5,000), and ends at `BATCH_PURGE_TIME` (20 s) or `BATCH_PURGE_CALLS` (100 calls, 500,000 rows); `BATCH_PURGE_JOBS` and `BATCH_PURGE_ROWS` are public so that the tests tie to them. A job that a pass began is therefore finished by the same pass unless it hit the time or the number of calls, and the one in-between state a person can read is pinned, through the store and over HTTP (`a_job_a_call_cut_short_still_reads_…`: `200`, the counters the job always had, the items it has left from the ordinal where the call stopped), and described in the runbook. The pass tests: 250 jobs without items are all gone in one pass (the continuation reads the job bound too), and a job of exactly one call's rows is finished by the same pass (the continuation is `>=`).
- **D2333** (2026-11-19, review round, correctness; T018-051 purge half) **The log of a failed call says the SQLSTATE and the kind of the error.** `StoreError::sqlstate()` keeps a code only for `Unavailable` and `Internal`, so a `23503` (`MissingReference`) logged `sqlstate="none"` and nothing else. `StoreError::kind()` is a new method of the application error: one closed word for each of its 14 variants (the `match` is exhaustive, so a new variant needs its word), never the text or the detail; the pass logs `kind` beside `sqlstate`. Tested by a unit test of the words and by two integration tests that break the purge on purpose, one with a trigger whose message quotes the reason of the job (`sqlstate="P0001" kind="internal"`, the canary in no log line and no metric) and one with a trigger that skips the delete of the items (`sqlstate="none" kind="missing_reference"`).
- **D2334** (2026-11-19, review round, security 1 and 2, architecture 4; G2321 closed) **`SagatBatchPurgeFailing`: `sum(increase(batch_purge_failures_total[1h])) > 0`, severity `warning`, no `for`.** One failure in an hour is a call that timed out or met a deadlock, which the next pass absorbs; one at every pass is the job at the head of the queue that cannot be removed, which blocks every other tenant's purge (G2323); a failure is an event and the increase stays an hour, as `SagatBatchSelectionFailed`. Written with `promtool` tests in `infra/observability/tests/batch_stalled_test.yaml` (the one file CI runs): the first failure at minute 5 (silent at 4, up at 10, gone at 90), a failure at every minute (up at 90), a purge that works (the counter of what it removed climbs, the one of its failures at zero: silent). Red first, with the rule absent: `got: []` at 10 and 90 minutes. Hand mutants of the rule, each restored byte for byte: another series (`batch_purged_total`), a window of one minute, the severity `critical`: all three fail the tests. Run with the Prometheus image pinned in `compose.observability.yaml` (`prom/prometheus:v3.15.0`) as the CI job mounts it (`check config`, `check rules`, `test rules` of the three files of `infra/observability/tests`). G2321's reason (`promtool` not installed) was wrong: it is available through the image. The rule is listed in `contract_observability_operations` (`SCHEDULE_RULES`), which checks that the metric is emitted and the runbook section exists.
- **D2335** (2026-11-19, review round, architecture 1, correctness 6; G2325) **The purge leaves the payload row of a `SIGNAL` or `UPDATE` job, and says so.** `batch_jobs_payload_fk` references `payloads (tenant_id, digest)`; `payloads` is content-addressed (a payload may be shared by digest, by this job and by others) and nothing in `crates/` collects the payloads that nothing references: that is the registry of feature 020 (G2316). So the purge removes the job and its items and not the payload, and the payload stays charged to the tenant (up to 256 KiB a job; bounded by `tenant_content_bytes`). The test (`the_payload_a_signal_job_holds_outlives_the_job_and_stays_charged`) pins what stays: the row is there after the purge, the usage is not refunded for it and is refunded for the job's selector and reason; the privacy canary (`the_prefix_and_the_reason_of_a_purged_job_…`) now carries a `SIGNAL` job whose body holds the canary and asserts that after the purge the canary is in `payloads` and in no other table, no log line, no span and no metric. A hand mutant that deletes the unreferenced payloads in the purge kills both.
- **D2336** (2026-11-19, review round, architecture 5) **The index follows the precedent of `20261017000000_scheduler_shards`.** `CREATE INDEX IF NOT EXISTS batch_jobs_purge_idx`, so that an operator may build it `CONCURRENTLY` first on a large `batch_jobs`, and a `DO $valid$` block that refuses an `INVALID` leftover of a failed concurrent build with a message that says what to do; the `down` drops it with `IF EXISTS`; the runbook has the recipe. `the_index_may_be_built_first_and_an_invalid_leftover_is_refused` builds the index first and applies (the index is not built again: the same oid), makes the index invalid (`pg_index.indisvalid`) and sees the migration refused with its message and no function left, drops it and applies. Red first: `relation "batch_jobs_purge_idx" already exists`.
- **D2337** (2026-11-20, G2280, T018-085) **`batch_jobs_purge(integer, integer)` joins the named definers of G2280.** The purge of batch jobs (#242, migration `20270119000000`) added a definer that pins `pg_catalog, public, pg_temp`, after ADR-0091 made `pg_catalog, pg_temp` the rule for new definers; the catalog test of this branch refused it on the merged tree (CI of PR #226, shard 3). It is safe for the same reason as the other three (the runtime role cannot create in `public`, and the test checks that), so it is named in the test beside them and in G2280, to be re-pinned with them; changing the function is a migration of the batch lane, not of this branch.
- **D2338** (2026-11-19, flaky tests of the batch lane under the whole suite) **A selection is awaited by its condition, never by a number of steps.** The selection of a batch job waits until every transaction that was open when the job was created has ended (`pg_snapshot_xmin(pg_current_snapshot()) > selection_horizon`), and that horizon is the whole PostgreSQL server's: every test of the binary has its own database on one server, so a transaction of another test holds it too. `a_replica_that_lost_its_lease_records_nothing_and_the_new_owner_goes_on` stepped 500 times a second of the test clock apart and then required `COMPLETED`; locally under the whole suite it found the job still `SELECTING`. The test now holds such a transaction itself across the creation, sees the new owner's step idle while it is open, ends it, and awaits the selection through `selected`, the helper `select` already used (a condition, with a 120 s bound that only keeps a defect from hanging the binary); the two 500-times-5-ms loops of `batch_pass.rs` that silently gave up after 2.5 s use it too. Seen red: the old loop with a transaction held across it ends `Selecting`, expected `Completed`.
- **D2339** (2026-11-19, flaky tests of the batch lane) **`select` brings the test's clock up to the job's first slot.** The first slot of a job is the database's `clock_timestamp()` at its creation, and `live` gave the test's clock a margin of two seconds over the wall clock: a job created after that margin was spent (the selection of the job before it waited for another test's transaction, D2340) had its slot ahead of the test's clock, every item step was `Idle`, `settle` returned and the job was `RUNNING` (`a_batch_rerun_keeps_the_identifier_unless_the_job_says_to_drop_it`, CI of PR #226 on `8170c334`, shard 3; its first selection waited about 110 s). The helper now moves the clock forward to the slot when it is behind, never back; it takes the fixture for that. `a_job_created_after_the_margin_of_the_test_clock_runs_once_it_is_selected` sets the clock a second behind the database's and was red (`Running`, expected `Completed`) before. The run-input permit pool of design 043 was ruled out for this failure: the workflow of the fixture has no input schema, so a rerun of it never asks for a permit.
- **D2340** (2026-11-19, flaky tests of the batch lane) **The takeover of `business_id_audit_platform.rs` returns once its update has the row or waits for it.** It returned as soon as its backend was known, before the update was sent. Under load the caller then committed the held binding first: the refused step read the old fence, recorded its decision, nothing ever waited for the takeover, and the stage "the record of the refusal waits for the fence" ran out its 120 s, while the takeover's open transaction, which has an identity, held the server's horizon (D2338), and with it the selection of every other test's batch job, for those 120 s. That is the CI failure of `a_rerun_item_that_finds_its_id_held_is_audited_once_by_the_engine_with_its_record`, and through D2339 the cause of the other one: both stalled tests went on within 0.2 s of its panic. Seen red: the old helper, its update let go only once the refused step had ended, fails the stage with the message CI printed (letting it go right after the binding committed was not enough to lose the race on an idle machine). The new helper cannot be driven into that order: it waits for the update before the binding is released. The reset-completion test of the same file uses the same helper.
- **D2341** (2026-11-19, flaky tests of the batch lane) **Two subscribers interested in nothing stay registered for the life of a test process.** `tracing-core` 0.1.36, while one dispatcher alone is registered, computes the interest of a call site from the default subscriber of whichever thread reaches the call site first (`Dispatchers::has_just_one`, `Rebuilder::JustOne`): a capture installed on one test's thread while no other is alive, and a call site reached first by another test's thread with no subscriber, caches that call site as never interesting, and the capture never hears it. That is why `the_prefix_and_the_reason_of_a_purged_job_reach_no_log_span_metric_or_audit_record_and_are_gone_afterwards` heard its probes but not the purge's own line under the whole suite. `tests/support/logs.rs` (`keep_interest_shared`, called by every capture, by the world and by the one test of this suite that installs its own subscriber) registers two `Deaf` dispatchers (interest never, level hint `OFF`), so the registry never has just one and every live subscriber is asked under its lock. `a_line_whose_call_site_another_thread_reached_first_is_heard_by_the_capture` was red alone before (no line heard).

- **D2405** (2026-11-26, G2403 option (c), FR-018-029, FR-018-028, the coordinating session's decision for release 0.3.0) **A job whose selector names its runs by id selects the runs of its namespace it names, up to `maxItems`, lowest run id first, with no time bound; a filter selector stays bounded by `selection_bound_at`.** The bound of D1033 exists to freeze the open-ended set a filter matches against the runs that commit after the job was created. A list of at most 1,000 run ids is already a closed set, so for it the bound added nothing but the defect of G2403: a run committed before the job, whose `created_at` is later than the bound because the database clock stepped back in between, was left out, and a job that named that very run selected nothing and ended `COMPLETED`. The selection round and the preview of a selector of run ids now pass no bound (the statement's `$11` is `NULL` for it, `Bind::bound` in `crates/infra-postgres/src/batch.rs`); a filter selector, a selector by business-id prefix among them, passes the creation instant as before (the preview, the instant it is read). **What still refuses a named run, and none of it reads a clock:** the tenant and the namespace of the job, in the same statement (a named run of another namespace or of another tenant never becomes an item, not even a skipped one that would tell it exists); the wait for every transaction that had an identity when the job was created (D1958); `maxItems` (1 to 100,000), which takes the lowest run ids of a list longer than it (the keyset by run id) and completes the job; and for each item the creator's authority, checked again (`run:batch` and the permissions of the action, D1019), and the action's own rules under the run lock (an ended run is `SKIPPED`, an id that names no run matches nothing). Who may see a run is not a rule of the selection, before or after this decision: a job is checked against its creator's permissions in the namespace. **What the bound did for a selector of run ids and no longer does:** leave out a named run whose recorded creation is later than the job. Such a run either committed before the job while the clock stepped back (the case of this decision) or came to exist after the job and before the round read it, which needs an id that is known before its run exists: the only such ids are the derived ids of child runs (`child_run_id`, from the parent run and the step). That run is taken when its id sorts above the selection's cursor when it appears, and missed when the cursor has passed it: at most the ids of the list, bounded, and either way the job ends. It is a run the person named, of the job's tenant and namespace, and every rule of an item applies to it. Telling the two cases apart without a clock needs the creating transaction of each run, which is option (b) of G2403, left open for the filter selectors. *Tests (red first):* `batch_selection_bound::a_job_that_names_a_run_by_id_selects_it_whatever_the_database_clock_says_of_its_creation` and `batch_selection_bound::an_id_selector_still_refuses_the_runs_of_another_namespace_or_tenant_whatever_their_creation` were red before the change (the job selected nothing, the preview counted nothing); `batch_selection_bound::a_filter_selector_still_leaves_out_a_run_recorded_after_its_bound` holds the bound of the filter and passed before and after; `batch_selection_bound::a_prefix_selector_still_leaves_out_a_run_recorded_after_its_bound` holds the bound of the business-id prefix branch, red under a mutation that made only that branch unbounded (review of pull request 294). The statement of a selector of run ids is its own text since D2406.
- **D2406** (2026-11-26, review of pull request 294, FR-018-029, FR-018-028, D2405) **A selector of run ids is selected and previewed by a statement of its own, `r.id = ANY($3)` on the primary key, and not by the text of the filter.** Until now the ids shared the filter's text, `($10::uuid[] IS NULL OR r.id = ANY($10))`, which is a condition of an index only in a plan made for the values bound. PostgreSQL may use a generic plan after five executions of a prepared statement, and a generic plan of that text cannot assume the list is there: it walks `runs_listing_idx` (tenant, namespace, id) and filters each run of the namespace by the list, so the last, short page of a job reads the rest of the namespace under the round's 10-second statement timeout. That is the reasoning D2191 used for the prefix. The new statements, `SELECTION_BY_IDS_SQL` (tenant, namespace, the ids, the cursor, the size of the page) and `PREVIEW_BY_IDS_SQL` (tenant, namespace, the ids, the most it reads), have no time condition at all, which is how D2405 is held now (the bound is no longer a parameter that may be empty); the filter's statements lose the list of ids and bind the bound always (`$10`), so a filter can never be unbounded by a parameter either. *Test:* `batch_selection_bound::a_list_of_run_ids_is_read_from_the_primary_key_even_by_a_generic_plan` prepares both statements under `SET plan_cache_mode = force_generic_plan` in a namespace of 6,000 runs and finds `runs_pkey` in each plan; it was red against the shared text (the plan walked `runs_listing_idx`).

## 4. Gaps

| ID | Gap | Reason / closure | Status |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| G1000 | Definition cron triggers keep G84 semantics; no policy fields in the trigger IR. | API schedules offer them; an IR extension needs its own publication/diff rules. | Accepted |
| G1001 | No reset point while a branch waits or inside an active scope; no reset of chain segments or child runs. | Per-kind re-arming rules need their own evidence. | Accepted |
| G1002 | No batch reset or deletion. | Reset needs a per-run choice; deletion is a retention decision (G42/G69). | Accepted |
| G1003 | No terminate variants; no "cancel then start" conflict policy. | No terminate exists; a start waiting for a drain is unbounded. | Accepted |
| G1004 | Nominal time reaches a run only through `nominalTimeField`. | A run-metadata expression namespace is an IR change. | Accepted |
| G1005 | Firing, batch rate and reset size ceilings are design values. | T018-072 benchmark on a declared environment. | Open |
| G1006 | `REJECT_DUPLICATE` lasts only for the reuse window. | Finite ceilings (ADR-0027); O1001. | Open (owner) |
| G1007 | An auto-paused schedule is shown in audit, state and a namespace alert, not sent to a person. | Notification channels are 026. | Accepted |
| G1008 | No shared holiday/exclusion calendar resource. | New scope. | Accepted |
| G1009 | Start delay with run-level concurrency was refused. | Closed by D1608: 022 FR-022-018 defines acquire-or-enqueue at release. | Closed |
| G1010 | 020's `RunSelector` section was missing. | Closed: `specs/020-visibility-analytics-archival/contracts/list-query.md` § `RunSelector` (co-owned with 018) now defines it. | Closed |
| G1011 | After the handover, rolling back to an engine without schedules re-enables the legacy cron pass, which ignores pause (the shared key still prevents duplicates); a registry-aware engine without `schedules-v1` refuses to run instead, so this applies only below the registry release (G1607). | Rollback runbook states it. | Accepted |
| G1012 | Shared files and numbers with the other open branches (checked 2026-10-29): the 014 Vacate branch (`feature/014-capacity-vacate`, PR #59) appends three audit actions to `crates/domain/src/audit.rs` and two lines to `crates/domain/src/execution/mod.rs`, as this feature does (a trivial textual conflict at the end of `AuditAction` and of `mod.rs`); migration `20261226000000` is taken by 014 (`capacity_transfer_receipts`) and reserved by 036, so this feature's migration takes a later timestamp chosen after a fresh grep of every branch (T018-008). | Resolve the conflicts on merge, keeping both sets; T018-008 re-greps `origin/*` and the sibling worktrees. | Open |
| G1013 | The `dst` policy of a spec (gap `SKIP`/`SHIFT_FORWARD`, ambiguous `ONCE_EARLIER`/`TWICE`) applies to its structured calendars. Cron strings keep the daylight-saving behaviour feature 004 gave them (the cron library's own rules), whatever the policy says, so moving a trigger to a schedule never changes when it fires. FR-018-002 reads as if `dst` covered every entry; the cron library does not expose its handling, and re-implementing the five-field grammar to apply the policy would risk the 004 behaviour. | Tested by `a_cron_string_keeps_the_feature_004_daylight_saving_behaviour`; the Studio form and the docs say the policy is for calendars. | Open (owner) |
| G1014 | `createSchedule`, `updateSchedule`, `deleteSchedule`, `pauseSchedule` and `resumeSchedule` require an `Idempotency-Key` (8 to 160 printable characters, like every operation that changes something) but do not replay by it: a request sent again after a lost answer is not answered with the original result. It is refused by what the first one did (409 for a name in use or a stale `expectedRevision`, 404 for a schedule that is gone; pause and resume are idempotent by their nature). Nothing is applied twice. | The spec asks for replay by key only for trigger-now and the with-start operations (FR-018-024, FR-018-026); a replay store for schedule writes needs its own table and retention (ADR-0050 decision 4). | Accepted |
| G1016 | D1040..D1045 (the Rust SDK, `CANCEL_OTHER`, the waiting reservation, the waiting order, the jitter seed, the item backoff) were written on this branch on 2026-10-29 in the numbers the parity branch gives feature 019 (its D1040..D1079). 018's own block (D1000..D1039) is full, and the check of that day looked at files and migrations but not at the decision blocks. Both sets are published, so neither is renumbered here. | The owner decides which side moves (suggested: 018's six become D1800..D1805 when PR #53 and PR #63 meet, with the references in code and tasks updated in the same change); new 018 decisions use D1800 and up meanwhile. | Open (owner) |
| G1017 | A waiting trigger-now counts as one of the remaining actions a scheduled time must leave (the reservation of D1042 counts every waiting action), though it never uses one up; with `remainingActions` set and a trigger-now waiting, a scheduled time can find the schedule exhausted a time early. | The fix is to count waiting scheduled actions only, which needs the waiting count by kind in `Standing`. **Closed 2026-11-10 (D2190):** done, and the gap was understated: nothing gave the time back either, so the schedule stayed `ACTIVE` with an action left and no next time for ever. | **Closed** |
| G1015 | `listSchedules` filters by `state`, `origin`, `workflowId` and the start of a `name` (done 2026-11-05, without a new index: a namespace holds at most as many schedules as its `schedules` quota, which bounds the scan over the head revisions, and the order and the cursor are those of the unfiltered listing, so a page ends where the next begins whichever part filters it). The response `action` still carries `inputDigest` but not `inputBytes`. | An index per filter column (`schedules_by_workflow`, `schedules_by_state`) is a migration for a scan the quota already bounds; it is worth adding if a namespace's ceiling is raised far above the default. | Open (`inputBytes` only) |
| G1018 | The crash-at-each-write matrix of T018-020 kills the transaction that starts a run (28 writes) and the one that records a skipped time (3 writes). The other decision-only steps (buffer, coalesce, drop, reconcile, auto-pause, the backfill step) have the same shape (fence, compare-and-swap, action row, new state in one transaction) but are not killed at each write; no test boots the control plane with schedules enabled and a skewed clock. | Extend the matrix with the `Step` enum of `tests/fault/schedules-bulk-operations/firing.rs` when a task touches those steps; add a control-plane wiring test with T018-018's remaining work. | *Closed in part 2026-11-10 (D2159):* the decision-only steps are killed at each write (12 cases; nothing found). Open: a test that boots the control plane with schedules enabled and a skewed clock |
| G1607 | A binary older than the instance-capability registry release does not register, so no activation can see it; activations therefore require the registry release deployed fleet-wide first (operator precondition, checked by nothing but the runbook), and rollback below a recorded requirement is unsupported. | Inherent to introducing the registry; 029's prerequisite release (T029-003) is the vehicle. | Accepted |
| G1605 | Draft lock orders that deviated from ADR-0050 decision 7 on 2026-10-27 (026 T2 membership and rate buckets after the link row; 030 endpoint head before memberships; 021 asset heads after secrets and the variable fence as an "authority slot"). Resolved the same day: 021 and 026 now follow decision 7 exactly (021 D1624, 026 D1337 revised); 030 follows it with one listed exception, a caller transaction locking its own `START` row after its run (030 D1660), recorded in decision 7. | Owners 021, 026, 030. | Resolved |
| G1606 | `REMATERIALIZE` checks of other features (retired version, paused resource, retired snapshot, busy permit) are evaluated at reset time; a preview reports them as provisional and a reset can still fail on them. | Inherent to reset at a later time. | Accepted |
| G1950 | A run keeps no record of whose authority it started under. The 017 admission of a database step claimed after the schedule was resumed by someone else (or after `authorizedBy` changed through an update) stands on the new `authorizedBy`, not on the member the run started under (D1950). | Fails closed when that member lacks database authority (the step is refused, the run is not corrupted), and open when the new one has it and the old one did not. Closing it needs a column on `runs` (an additive migration with the erasure-aware immutability trigger of the provenance columns) or a different rule for the claim. | Open |
| G1951 | The count of a recovery's summary stops after 100 calls of the calendar (a million times: a year of a schedule every 30 s, the densest the spacing floor and the longest window allow). Past it the step records a partial `COALESCED` summary and the recovery is remembered with no actions left, so the next step goes on summarizing instead of acting again; the work per step is bounded, the number of steps is not. | A schedule that dense with a window that long and an outage that long is not a case anyone sized for; the steps are cheap (one small transaction each) and the pass takes `steps_per_schedule` of them per round. Revisit with the benchmark (T018-072). | Open |
| G1953 | A waiting action keeps waiting however long the runs that hold it back stay open (a run in `MANUAL_REVIEW` after a failed compensation can stay open for days) and starts when they end, even if it is older than the catch-up window; `data-model.md` has it become `MISSED_CATCHUP_WINDOW` instead. | Starting a stale time is the safe direction (the work is not lost) and the action is visible as `BUFFERED`; the transition needs an update of an action row under the append-only guard. | Open |
| G1954 | Only the actions are purged. | G1957 | A transaction that began before a batch job was created and takes its identity only after it can commit a run whose `created_at` is before the job's bound after the selection read, and the run is not selected (D1958). | It is concurrent with the creation of the job, which commits first, so the run is not "before" the job in any order a caller can observe; the window is the milliseconds between reading the clock and the first write of a run creation. | G1991 | An entry that waits cannot be withdrawn, and does not expire (D2000). Everything that follows from that: (1) a source that never ends (a run in manual review nobody looks at) holds one of the namespace's places (16 by default) for as long as it lives; (2) the person who asked has no call that gives the place up, and **an administrator has none either**; (3) **revoking the requester does not free the place**: the engine fails the entry (`AUTHORITY_REVOKED`) only when the source has ended, so the one lever on an entry that waits is to end the source (retry, skip or resolve the step), or to remove the member and wait for it to end; (4) the limit can be raised, but only up to the hard ceiling of 64 (`quota_ceilings`), so a namespace in which sources are stuck can be filled for good; (5) the customer's run was cancelled by the request and stays cancelled whatever becomes of the entry. **Amended 2026-11-09 (OD-2026-11-08-1, D2059 to D2068): the expiry is decided and built, so (1) and (4) no longer hold for good: a place is held for the window at most (24 hours by default, 1 hour to 7 days), after which the entry ends `FAILED` `SOURCE_DID_NOT_END` and the place is free; (3) now has a second lever, waiting out the window. (2) The clause "a prefix instance compensated, skipped, resolved or reconciled later" cannot be reached by a journal the reducer accepts: a compensation exists only inside a saga, whose node is parked while its body runs, so every point before a compensated instance is already refused for a waiting step. The clause is implemented and its predicate is unit-tested, but no end-to-end journal exercises its wiring. (3) The corpus of `tests/reset.rs` has no approval run and no loop run, for (1): their completions are not candidates; approval decisions are covered where real approval flows exist (T018-055). | (1) widening the points is a product choice (more places to reset to) and the owner decides it; (2) is a defence for event shapes a later feature may add. | Open (owner, for 1) |
| G1978 | Two consequences of D1975 for the reset are not settled. (1) The retained arguments are on the receipt of the run that applied the Update. A reset re-emits the prefix of the source with its `retained: true` markers under a new run, so a reset of that reset run finds `retained: true` rows whose arguments sit on the receipts of the source: the reset must follow `reset_of` to the source's receipts, or copy the references. (2) A Signal-with-Start whose signal reaches an open holder is recorded `signal_with_start`, as the contract's table says, so a reset with `SIGNALS` (which re-delivers `via: signal`) does not deliver it again, although it is a signal to an open run; delivering it would make the reset re-signal what the caller asked for in one request that also may have started a run. | Both belong to the reset (T018-053, T018-054, T018-056): the rows are written now so the reset can choose; changing what `via` says later would need a rewrite of history. The reset reports a row it cannot reapply (`NOT_REAPPLIED`) with a reason. | **Closed 2026-11-06 (D1982):** (1) the arguments are found by following the resets that made the run; (2) a `signal_with_start` row is not delivered again. |
| G1977 | The `reapplyOnReset` capability (D1974) has no setting of a deployment: `set_reset_reapply` is called by no binary, so a workflow that opts in cannot be published anywhere yet. A client cannot know the capabilities of a server (ADR-0032, consequences), and the refusal names the field. | **Closed 2026-11-06 (D1986, D1990):** `SAGAT_RESET_REAPPLY_ENABLED` in the control plane (the engine has none: it reads stored versions with `parse_stored`); `sagat check` still has no deployment and accepts what a server without the flag refuses. |
| G1974 | Two cases of Update-with-Start (D1970) are not proved or are weaker than the clause. (1) With an open holder the Update runs in a transaction after the claim of the identifier has ended, not under it: if the holder closes in between, the answer is `409 RUN_NOT_MUTABLE`, not a new run. (2) A holder that is a continuation chain is served in the segment that is current when the claim ends (`current_segment`); if that run hands off to its successor between that read and the effect, the answer is `409 RUN_CONTINUED` or `NOT_WAITING`. And a new run that 022's permit would queue is not rolled back, for the reason of G1973 (2). | (1) and (2) are races the caller sees as a typed 409 that a retry resolves, and they are the answers `updateRun` gives to the same race; closing them needs the Update inside the claim's transaction for an existing holder too, which the binding lock order (ADR-0050) allows and a later slice may do. | Open |
| G1952 | A backfill step that the admission refuses (a full quota, no published version) waits and is tried again on every round of the pass, with no backoff, unlike a scheduled time (D1952). | A round is one poll of the database per owned shard and the step is one rolled-back transaction; harmless at the default poll but wasteful. Fix with a `retry_at` on the backfill (a migration). | Open |
| G1996 | A row of `serving_instances` was owned by knowing its random `instance_id`, which the runtime role can read: another process holding the runtime role could refresh, delete or rewrite it. | *Closed 2026-11-08 (D2024):* a row is touched only for the token that registered it, and the runtime role cannot read the hash. What a holder of the runtime role can still do is G2000. | Closed |
| G1997 | The capability was the binary's, not the setting's: an engine with `SAGAT_SCHEDULES_ENABLED` unset listed `schedules-v1` and ran no schedule pass, so the handover (T018-061) would have stopped the legacy cron pass in an engine that fires no schedule. | *Closed 2026-11-08 (D2023):* an engine lists `schedules-v1` only when its configuration runs the schedule pass; an activation refuses a live engine without it, naming its id. | Closed |
| G1998 | The engine's worker gRPC API could have kept answering while its passes were stopped by a requirement it lacks. | *Closed 2026-11-08 (D2021, D2031):* the admission layer of the gRPC server answers every new call `UNAVAILABLE` with `x-sagat-error: BINARY_CAPABILITY_MISSING` before it authenticates while the gate is closed, and the producer of a task stream checks the gate before every claim, so a stream open when the gate closes leases nothing more (it ends with that answer at its next claim, within its window of 5 s); a worker goes to another replica. A frame already queued is delivered. | Closed |
| G1999 | The registry does not carry `SAGAT_BATCHES_ENABLED`, `SAGAT_RESET_REAPPLY_ENABLED` and `SAGAT_RESET_CANCEL_SOURCE_ENABLED`: no capability is defined for them. G1965, G1988 and G1989 are therefore unchanged: with the flag on in the control plane and off in every engine the registry does not refuse. | Moving them is a change of its own (a capability value, a migration that widens the CHECKs, the engines listing it, the activation commands) that supersedes ADR-0081, which says so in its amendment of 2026-11-08. | Open |
| G2000 | What a holder of the runtime role can still do to the registry, which no function of the database can close because the role legitimately calls them: (1) register up to 1,000 rows of its own, so that every new process finds the table full (it serves, uncounted: D2025) and an activation's verification is incomplete; (2) hold the registration lock open (the epoch `FOR SHARE` and the public advisory lock that counts the table, in a transaction it does not finish), so that new processes time out and stay refused until it lets go; (3) fail a victim's heartbeat by holding its row lock, which closes the victim's gate after 30 s (D2026); (4) at the cap, restarts evict nothing while the rows are live. | G2001 | Feature 029's request path reads the epoch row `FOR SHARE` inside tenant transactions, but `sagat_app` has `SELECT` only on `platform_requirement_epoch` and a share lock needs `UPDATE`; a share lock held for a whole tenant transaction would also make a queued activation's `FOR UPDATE` wait, and that wait stalls the registration of every new process. | Not built for 029 (not approved). It needs a definer function the runtime role can execute that *reads* the epoch (and does not hold it), and its own proof that the wait of an activation cannot starve new registrations. A dated note is in 029's `plan.md` and `data-model.md`. | Open (029) |
| G2002 | What the activation command of a feature (`control-plane schedule-trigger-handover`, T018-061) must also do: (1) take `--expect-live N` and refuse when fewer than N live instances of the required kinds exist (an activation with none passes vacuously); (2) map `permission denied for function` to "run with the owner role"; (3) say in `cli.rs` which commands need the owner role (the usage text now names the activation commands of the instance registry). | Not built here: T018-061 owns the command. | Open (T018-061) |
| G2003 | A precondition for activating 029's `access-narrowing-v1` or 031's `payload-encoding-v1`: the whole fleet runs builds with the fail rule of D2026 (a heartbeat that fails for longer than the liveness window for a reason other than a lost connection closes the gate), with the token of D2024, and an engine's capabilities follow its configuration (D2023). | G2004 | The Studio could not show the pause, resume and run-now buttons of a trigger-owned schedule as disabled before the handover. | *Closed 2026-11-08 (D2053):* the schedule resource carries `controllable` for a `TRIGGER` schedule; the Studio disables the buttons with the reason. | Closed |
| G2005 | An engine binary older than the registry release neither registers nor reads the handover: after it, it keeps its legacy cron pass, which starts no tick twice (shared key) but **ignores a pause** of a trigger-owned schedule (G1011, said in the runbook, section 6.4; test `upgrade::an_old_engine_binary_keeps_firing_after_the_handover…`). | G2006 | For the seconds between a publication and the reconciliation's next round a new trigger-owned schedule has no next time (publication has no calendar), and while the state is `LEGACY` the legacy pass plans the trigger's first time by its own clock: the first tick of a new trigger is fired by whichever pass planned it, and the schedule's next time is the trigger's own when it has one. | A tick that is fired by one pass only is still fired once. | Accepted |
| G2007 | A trigger whose four possible schedule names a tenant took in advance has no schedule (`MISSING` every round, logged with tenant and trigger, backing off to five minutes); the legacy pass still fires it. | Only the tenant that made the names suffers it. | Accepted |

| G2014 | *(Raised 2026-11-09 and withdrawn the same day: it said that the control plane writes `run_resets.created_at` with its own process clock, so a skewed control plane would move every expiry. | G2043 | **A lone batch job applies about one item a second, not the 20 it asks for by default** (and 75 a second, not 200, at the highest rate it may ask for). The executor never catches up in a burst, by design (`next_slot = max(slot, now) + interval`, "a job that ran late never catches up"), one step applies at most one item of a job, and the engine's loop rests `SAGAT_BATCHES_POLL_MS` (1,000 ms) whenever a round found no job due: after an item the job's next slot is 50 ms away, the next round finds nothing due, and the loop sleeps. Measured (3 of 3 repetitions, 120 runs, the release build): 0.97 to 0.98 items a second, the gap between two items 1,036 ms at the median; with a poll of 50 ms 13.3 to 13.6 items a second (on a machine at load 15 to 24); sixteen jobs at once get 4.5 items a second each, because the other jobs keep the loop busy, so **the rate of a job depends on how many other jobs there are**. The runbook calls the poll the "pause when a round found nothing" and the rate "one item per slot" and does not say the two together bound a lone job at one item per poll. | The loop should wait until the earliest `next_slot_at` of a due job, bounded by the poll, instead of the whole poll: a small change of `run_batches` / `BatchPass::round` that needs its own decision and test (both roles). Or the runbook says it. Not changed here (D2112). The bounds "reaches 80% of the rate it asked for" are broken in 3 of 3 repetitions at both rates. | **Closed 2026-11-09 for the lone job (D2116):** the round says when the soonest slot of a job comes and the loop wakes then; the same scenario now applies 18.4 items a second (the bound held in 6 of 6 repetitions; addendum of the report). The two bounds that stay broken are the replica's capacity, not the loop (G2044, D2121). |
| G2044 | **One engine replica starts about 60 runs a second, and a burst is as late as its size over that.** 10,000 schedules due at the same instant: the lag at p50 82 s, p95 158 s, max 166 s (60 actions a second; 3 of 3 repetitions broke the bound of 60 s at the p95); with two replicas on the same database 131 to 135 actions a second and p95 70 s, max 74 s; spread evenly over a minute (167 a second) p95 100 s with one replica; at 1,000 schedules p95 16 s at the same instant and 1.0 s spread over a minute. The round ceilings do not change it: `SAGAT_SCHEDULES_ROUND_ACTIONS` 1,000 with `SAGAT_SCHEDULES_BATCH` 500 gives p50 82 s, p95 157 s, max 165 s and 60.6 a second, with rounds of 8.3 s instead of 1.6 s (a ceiling bounds the length of a round and so the fairness search, not the rate). An action costs about 16 ms of which a commit is 0.5 ms (a sequence of round trips; not taken apart); one replica applies about 75 batch items a second to all its jobs together (13 ms an item, 7.4 ms of it the action). `SagatSchedulerLate` (60 s for 5 minutes) would not fire in any repetition (the gauge was above 60 s for 110 s at most), though the gauge the replicas scale on would. | The scaling runbook should say what the numbers say: N schedules (or batch items) due together are late by about N / 60 s (N / 75 s) per replica, two replicas about halve it, and 10,000 schedules of a per-minute spec need three replicas or more. A change of the pass (parallel firing inside a replica) is a design, not a setting. Nothing changed here. | **Said in the runbook 2026-11-09 (D2119)**: `docs/runbooks/schedules.md` 4.1 has the table and the extrapolation, as measurements of one machine. A design for firing in parallel stays open. |
| G2045 | **A burst of schedules in one namespace meets the default quota of 10,000 active runs, and the pass goes on refusing what it cannot admit.** The first launch of the benchmark at 10,000 schedules (the runs never finish: no worker) stopped at 9,998 schedules acted; the engine answers a refused admission with `ADMISSION_RETRY` and tries again, and the pass spent its rounds on the second time of every schedule it could not admit (10,500 refusals in 426 s, 50 a second, no end). Nothing says so: the schedules runbook does not name `ADMISSION_RETRY` or the quota, and `SagatSchedulerLate` fires on the lag that results, not on the cause. The benchmark raises the quota (`active_runs` and `tenant_active_runs`) for every scenario. | Name the quota and `ADMISSION_RETRY` in `docs/runbooks/schedules.md` ("a schedule did not start a run" list). Not changed here. (The rate in the first sentence is wrong: 10,500 refusals in 426 s are 25 a second, not 50.) | **Closed in the runbook 2026-11-09 (D2119)**: `schedules.md` 3 and 4.1 name the quota, `ADMISSION_RETRY`, the metric and what the operator sees. |
| G2046 | **A reset holds the row of its source run for the whole re-emission, about 0.93 ms for every event of the prefix.** The source row is locked from the start of the creation transaction to its commit: 0.95 s at 1,000 events, **4.65 s at 5,000** (`RESET_PREFIX_EVENTS`, the largest prefix accepted; 3 of 3 repetitions broke the bound of 1 s; the reset as a whole 4.7 s, within the 5 s bound by 0.15 to 0.24 s), and about 19 s at the hard ceiling of 20,000 that the plan names (a linear extrapolation: no code path accepts 20,000 today). Every other writer of that run (a cancel, a retention sweep, another reset) waits that long. The restore writes the events one statement at a time (the transaction is 5,000 round trips). | Write the restored rows in pages (one statement for hundreds of events) or lower the ceiling: a decision with the numbers above, tested in both roles; `RESET_PREFIX_EVENTS` is left at 5,000. Do not raise the ceiling toward 20,000 before that. | Open. The runbook says what it means for a person (D2120, `run-reset.md` 2.1); the restore is still written one statement at a time. |
| G2047 | **The points, the preview and the reset replay the whole journal of the source, and the replay costs more than linearly in its length, not in the length of the prefix.** `reset_points` 9 ms at 1,002 events, 89 ms at 5,002, 1,313 ms at 20,002 (3 of 3 repetitions: 1,273 to 1,328); the preview of the same prefix of 5,000 events 98 ms on a journal of 5,002 and 1,367 ms on one of 20,002; the reset of it 4.7 s and 6.0 s (the extra 1.3 s is before the transaction). Reading the journal is linear and cheap (the refusal of a point past the ceiling takes 46 ms at 20,002 events, in the preview and in the reset: it is answered before the replay, and creates no run); `verify_run` of a run a reset made takes 20 ms at 5,000 events and replay equals projection in every one of the 15 runs the resets made (5 for each of 3 repetitions at each size). | A journal of hundreds of thousands of events would make the page that lists the points a multi-second request: look at `reset_points` / `reset_plan` before the point list is shown for long runs. Not changed here. | Open. The runbook says what it means for a person (D2120, `run-reset.md` 2.1); the replay is unchanged. |
| G2048 | **The reconciliation of trigger schedules drains a backlog at 30 to 77 schedules a second, not at the 100 it advertises, and 100,000 triggers take 21 to 27 minutes, not the 17 of the estimate (G2023).** Measured with two tenants and the engine's own loop for 60 s: at 100,000 triggers 62 a second under the default settings (20 an entry a tenant, 39.6 writes in a round of 209 ms, then a pause of 400 ms: 27 minutes) and 77 a second with the per-tenant cap at 100 (193 writes in a round of 677 ms, then a pause of 1.9 s: 21 minutes); at 10,000 triggers 30 and 69 a second (42 to 46 rounds that wrote and 7 or 8 rounds that found nothing to write, each followed by the poll of 5 s: the sweep over so few triggers restarts and the loop rests). The pause that bounds the rate (10 ms a write) is added to the time of the round, so the pace can only be below 100. The convergence count (one statement over every shard, capped at 100,000) took 194 ms at the median with 100,000 unconverged and 6 ms at 10,000; the first window of a sweep 46 ms (max 336 ms) and 7 ms; every statement far inside the 4 s timeout; no write failed or was refused. | The estimate of G2023 is replaced by these figures; the runbook's "about 3 minutes for 10,000 triggers of one tenant" is about 6 at 2 tenants under the defaults. No change. | **Measured; the documents are corrected 2026-11-09 (D2118)**: ADR-0083, `schedules.md` and the notes of D2047 and G2023 say "at most 100 a second" and the measured range. |
| G2049 | **The check the handover makes under its lock depends on the plan the statistics give it, and took 2.85 s at 10,000 triggers.** `schedule_trigger_unconverged()` (an `EXISTS` over the drift view) stops at the first row when there is one and must look at every trigger when there is none, which is the state the handover needs. On a converged fleet its median was 1,009 ms at 5,000 triggers (3 of 3 repetitions: 999 to 1,014 ms), **2,853 ms at 10,000** (2,851 to 2,856 ms), 28 ms at 15,000, 43 ms at 20,000, 111 ms at 50,000 and 280 ms at 100,000: at 5,000 and 10,000 the planner chose a nested loop that rescans `schedules` for each trigger (a sequential scan of `schedules` under a `Materialize`, 99,990,000 rows removed by the join filter in the plan of one repetition at 10,000, kept in the fragment) and from 15,000 on it used `schedules_trigger_uniq`. The plan that was chosen at 10,000 is quadratic, so a fleet that gets it at a larger size would exceed the statement timeout of 4 s (the same plan at 14,000 triggers would take about 5.6 s: an extrapolation, not measured). The convergence count and the windows of the reconciliation did not show it (11 ms and 14 ms at 10,000). | Look at the plan of the check (and of the view under `EXISTS`) at sizes between 5,000 and 20,000 and make it independent of the statistics (a lateral or a `NOT EXISTS` form, or an index-forcing rewrite) with its own decision and test; the property the handover needs is "within the timeout at every size", which this run shows at the six sizes it measured (5,000 to 100,000) and not between 10,000 and 15,000, where the plan changes. Not changed here. | **Closed 2026-11-09 (D2117)**: the view looks the schedule up for each trigger, in place in `20270114000000_trigger_handover`; the check took 19.7 ms at 10,000 triggers (2,853 ms before), 10 ms at 5,000 (1,009 ms) and 263 ms at 100,000 (280 ms) (addendum of the report). The cause was not the statistics of the bulk-inserted tables (they were analyzed) but the singleton `schedule_trigger_handover`, never analyzed, and the planning of an `EXISTS` for its first row. |
| G2050 | **What this benchmark did not measure, and why.** The selection of 100,000 runs (the hard `maxItems`): starting 100,000 runs that never finish takes ten minutes here, and 10,000 took 0.3 s (31,000 runs a second); the cross-tenant fairness of the batch and the schedule passes (one tenant of one namespace in every scenario but the trigger ones, which have two); the batch pass on two replicas; the claim across processes (one process with a pool of 48 held the 32); the HTTP API in front of any of it (authentication, the rate limiter); workers that run what is started (the load of a real platform is more than the engine passes); a reset with reapplied signals or Updates, with a business id, `CANCEL_SOURCE` and its pass, and a reset at a prefix that the ceiling would accept above 5,000; backfills and catch-up storms; the delayed-start release pass; PostgreSQL on another machine or another version; any run longer than the hour each scenario takes; and the effect of the load of the other work on this machine (the figures vary by a few percent between repetitions, which the brackets show, and the load of the 50 ms poll variant was three times that of the others). | A run on a quiet machine with the database on another, and the scenarios above, belong to the release gate of a feature that makes a promise; NFR-018-005 makes none. | Accepted |
| G2051 | **The wake-up of the batch loop (D2116) covers the slot of a job and nothing else, and costs one idle round after each item.** A job whose selection waits for a transaction, and a job whose items are all deferred (the retry delay starts at 5 s and doubles), have no slot ahead, so they are still looked at once a poll (`SAGAT_BATCHES_POLL_MS`, 1 s): a deferred item can be retried up to a poll after it may be. And the round that applies an item is followed by one round that finds nothing due (a clock read and the due scan, which since D2123 also answers the slot ahead), so a lone job at the default rate costs one round more for each item than it would if the round that applied the item knew the slot; the work round does not ask because the next one follows at once and may find more due (a job that selects a page and applies an item in the same round, a round that stopped at its ceiling). | The round that worked and found no due job left behind could return the slot it has just written (`record_item` returns the job); not done: it is an optimisation of a statement a second, and the fairness argument for the idle round is simpler to hold. A wake-up for the retry time of a deferred item is a design of its own. | Accepted |
| G2052 | **The check the handover makes under its lock is now linear in the number of cron triggers, and nobody measured a fleet bigger than 100,000.** After D2117 it is one index probe for each trigger (about 2.6 microseconds: 263 ms at 100,000, 20 ms at 10,000), which, by extrapolation and not by measurement, would reach the 4 s statement timeout at about 1.5 million triggers; the lock timeout of 2 s is the writers' wait and does not bound the check once the lock is held. | G2053 | **Another statement joins the unanalyzed singleton `schedule_trigger_handover` with a plain `CROSS JOIN`.** (Not fixed here: it has `LIMIT 20` and a selective filter, `h.state = 'HANDED_OVER'` and `last_fire_at` after the handover over a partial index, so it cannot be quadratic the way the view was; and the same remedy for the singleton's missing statistic, `ANALYZE` or a seeded `reltuples`, was rejected by D2117 because the view no longer depends on it.) `legacy_fires` (`crates/infra-postgres/src/trigger_schedules.rs`, the tripwire of an old engine binary, run every round that reads the state) reads `workflow_triggers t CROSS JOIN schedule_trigger_handover h` with `h.state = 'HANDED_OVER'` and a partial index on `last_fire_at`; the table is estimated at 610 rows, as in G2049. | G2054 | **The window of a slot that comes while the due scan runs is held by a seam, not by the production call.** The statement reads the clock once at its start; `PgBatchStore::due_jobs_as_of(shards, limit, Some(at))` gives it the instant, and the test takes the instant before a slot comes. The production call passes none, so what the test cannot show is that the statement reads the clock at the start and not later (a mutant that took the bound of the slot ahead from the later clock survives the pass-level test and is killed only by the seam test); a lock cannot hold the statement back after the clock is read, because the relation locks are taken before execution. | A statement that sleeps for the test (a function in the schema for tests) would hold it with no seam; not warranted. | Accepted |
| G2055 | **A due job that the executor keeps finding not due makes the loop look again every 10 ms.** `DUE_BUT_NOT_YET` is a hundred rounds a second at the most (a clock read, the scan and a step each), and only while the engine's clock reads before the slot the database's clock calls due: by half a round trip and a little drift in the ordinary case, which ends at the first or second look. A `DatabaseClock` that cannot sync (it keeps the last difference and says so in a warning) and has drifted by seconds would be this case for as long as the outage lasts, where it used to be one look a poll. Nothing reports the version of the zone database a replica holds, and nothing compares it between replicas: in a rolling upgrade across a release of the database (a country moving its change of daylight saving) the old and the new replica disagree for a time, each acting under the lease it holds, so a nominal time is acted on once, at the instant of whoever holds the shard. | Exposing `chrono_tz::IANA_TZDB_VERSION` in the engine's start-up log and in a metric label, and an upgrade note, are the cheap closures; whether a replica should refuse a shard of a schedule whose zone changed between builds is a product choice.
